# Plan

The working checklist for building Lumia. Section 18 of [architecture.md](architecture.md) gives the order and why; this file tracks what is done, what is next, and what changed along the way. Update it when work lands, not after.

Legend: `[x]` done · `[~]` in progress · `[ ]` not started · `(spec §n)` the section a task implements · `(tests)` the failure-injection tests that close it.

## Decisions and changes along the way

Things that moved from the first draft, with the reason. The architecture records the current decision; this list records that it changed.

| Was | Now | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Open core with a commercially licensed `ee/` directory | One AGPL edition, revenue from hosted plans (hard rule 6) | A second edition splits the codebase and the trust message; hosted plans can carry the business. |
| Home host per identity, DM-only guest accounts to reach strangers | Personal DMs live where they were started; the flagship is global because everyone has an account there | The home-host machinery served only two people on isolated instances who refuse the flagship. Recorded as deferred. |
| One identity private key copied to every device | Root key offline, per-device keys, signed roster with certificates | With identity-proof sign-in, a copied root key makes a stolen device a permanent takeover. MLS wants per-client keys anyway. |
| Roster signed as a whole, verified against history | Self-certifying roster: every device entry and revocation carries its own signature | An instance seeing version 10 first cannot verify the signer was ever authorized without every earlier version. |
| `guest` as an account kind | `guest` as a membership kind | One account can be an employee in one server and a contractor in another. |
| Content-free silent push wakeups | Visible push with generic text, on-device fetch and decrypt in the notification extension | iOS throttles and drops silent pushes; they cannot carry a timely notification. |
| Subresource integrity as the web E2EE defense | Origin-scoped web device keys; the origin operator is the trust boundary | SRI hashes are set by the party serving the HTML. |
| One Phoenix topic per chat channel | One Phoenix channel per socket, chat subscriptions as PubSub entries in that process | 100k sockets × 50 topics is 5M processes. |
| OpenMLS on the server through Rustler for delivery-service duties | No MLS library on the server; the delivery service stores opaque bytes and compares the epoch integer the client puts in the envelope | None of the duties parse an MLS message, and a server that cannot parse ciphertext cannot be asked to. |
| Web MLS keys as non-extractable WebCrypto objects where possible | Keys in WASM memory while the page runs; the OPFS store encrypted at rest under a WebCrypto key | WebCrypto has no HPKE; protecting one key while the rest sit in WASM memory is a claim the product could not make honestly. |
| Svelte for the web UI (proposed in the step 6 grill) | SolidJS 1.9 with Vite, TanStack Solid Router, Solid Query and Solid Virtual; bun as package manager and script runner | The design system's 36 components exist as React TSX in the design artifact and port to Solid mechanically (JSX kept, hooks become signals); TanStack Router has a Solid package and no Svelte one; Solid and Svelte 5 are equals on a weak webview. React itself was rejected: the components being React is not a reason to carry its runtime. |
| libcluster for multi-node (architecture 15.1 as first written) | `dns_cluster`, already in the instance: one DNS name with one record per node | Kubernetes headless services, Fly and scaled Compose all provide that record set; libcluster's other strategies (multicast gossip, static Epmd lists) do not apply to the deployments the install guide describes, and the tests connect nodes directly. |
| Electron for desktop (considered against Tauri in the step 6 grill) | Tauri 2 | Electron's bundled Chromium costs 100 to 150 MB idle against the 150 MB budget and buys only Linux consistency; Tauri keeps the Rust core in-process as an option and the Rust-native UI path as the Linux escape hatch. |
| Rust-native desktop UI (Slint, Iced, GPUI) as the escape hatch if WebKitGTK misses budget | Qt 6 with Qt Quick through cxx-qt, for Windows and Linux together, replacing `apps/desktop` rather than joining it (architecture §13, 2026-11-02) | The Rust-native toolkits were rejected for rich text, IME and accessibility, which Qt has; Telegram Desktop proves a fast Qt chat client on both platforms. Qt on Windows alone buys little over WebView2, but Qt on Linux beside Tauri on Windows is two desktop shells for one platform class. Tauri stays until the Linux budget row misses. |
| SwiftUI on macOS and the web UI in Tauri on Windows and Linux (architecture §13 as first written) | One desktop shell in Rust on Rune for all three, macOS first; the two shells it replaces ship until it reaches parity on their platforms (architecture §13, 2026-11-11) | Two desktop interfaces to keep alike, the Linux one on the weakest webview, and the core as WASM on two of three desktops. Two spikes built the same shell over a seeded core store on Rune and on GPUI: on macOS both cleared the client budgets of §17 several times over ([spikes/2026-11-11-rune-desktop.md](spikes/2026-11-11-rune-desktop.md), [spikes/2026-11-11-gpui-desktop.md](spikes/2026-11-11-gpui-desktop.md)). |
| Qt 6 with Qt Quick as the desktop escape hatch, on the Linux budget trigger | GPUI with GPUI Kit as the named fallback for the Rune shell, on a failed hand check or a missed desktop budget; Qt the last resort (architecture §13, 2026-11-11) | The spike showed GPUI steadier and older than Rune (every frame on time, its own input-method code per platform, selection across messages as shipped) at two to five times the CPU per frame and a supply chain three parties deep. Rune is the one being built on. The session layer of the shell names no toolkit type, so a move rewrites views. |
| Selection across messages in the Rune shell by a 13-line patch carried on Rune's Markdown widget (tried in the spike, 2026-11-11) | Lumia's own renderer for message bodies over Rune's rich text | Rune takes no pull requests, so the patch was a fork to carry for good. The renderer draws only the protocol's subset, leaves everything else as typed, costs the same per frame, and makes the copy a transcript. |
| Each replaced desktop shell ships until the Rune shell reaches parity on its platform, hand checks included (architecture §13 as amended that morning) | Both removed at once: the Tauri shell, and the SwiftUI macOS app with the Rune shell taking its bundle identifier, `lumia:` scheme and `appcast.xml` feed (Ryan, 2026-11-11) | No installs but testers', so there was nobody to protect from a gap, and keeping them meant three desktop codebases, two release paths and a webview's origins in the instance's CORS rule. The cost is written in architecture 13: the Mac app that ships has not passed its hand checks, and Windows and Linux have the browser until the shell is packaged there. |
| The Rune shell's updater left open between Sparkle on macOS and a signed manifest of Lumia's own on all three (plan step 9 as first written) | One appcast format and one key everywhere; Sparkle on macOS, WinSparkle on Windows, a small updater of Lumia's own for the Linux AppImage, the package manager for deb and rpm (architecture §13, 2026-11-11) | Checking and trusting an update can be the same on every desktop; replacing a running app cannot, and macOS and Windows each have a mature tool that reads the same feed. Lumia writes only the Linux one. |
| JS owns the store on web (what `lumia-wasm`'s `Session` surface assumed) | Rust owns the store on web too: rusqlite builds for wasm32 through `sqlite-wasm-rs`, so the native store, MLS key-value store, DM engine and identity flows run unchanged in a worker; JS supplies the UI, the socket and HTTP (confirmed by the step 6 spike, see below) | Zero porting of the E2EE and sync code and one store schema everywhere; the alternative was a second engine in JS or an effects rewrite. |
| Effect for the web client's JS | Decided by the spike: if Rust owns the transport loop in the worker as well, JS is a thin bridge and Effect is not used; if JS owns reconnect, refresh and worker lifecycle, Effect is used there and nowhere else | Its typed errors, retries and scoped resources fit a transport runtime and nothing else in this UI. Hono has no place: there is no JS server. |
| Brand assets under the repo licence (as first committed) | The Lumia name, logo and wordmark are excluded from the AGPL and Apache licences: all rights reserved by Ryan Winchester, with `assets/brand/LICENSE` granting display in unmodified builds (self-hosted instances included) and referential use; forks replace them. Icons and tokens stay under the software licence because the code depends on them. | A software licence grants no trademark rights, but the AGPL would have licensed the logo artwork itself; the carve-out is the usual pattern (Mastodon, Element, Gitea). |
| Bare `Phoenix.Socket.Transport` (proposed during the delivery grill) | Phoenix Channel with a custom serializer; `Hello` is the join, no envelope on the wire | Keeps Presence, `ChannelTest`, and `phoenix.js`; the serializer removes the framing cost. |
| "Cursor expired" meaning purged rows | Event rows permanent; "no longer served" is a 1,000-event replay cap | Permanent rows were already decided; the cap is what actually bounds replay cost. |
| `acl_version` bumped on every membership change | Bumped on definition-level changes only; member-level changes travel the account log | Bumping every channel on every role assignment is a re-resolution storm. |
| Flagship as mandatory identity provider (considered) | Key-based identity, flagship directory optional | Self-hosters would depend on Lumia's uptime to log in. |
| `apps/server` | `apps/instance` | "Server" means a community in Lumia. |
| `--binary-id` generator flag | Repo migration defaults plus `Lumia.Schema` | Same effect, and the schema module is where the UUIDv7 choice lives anyway. |
| `last_seq_at` indexed at microsecond precision | Second granularity, `fillfactor = 70` on `channels` | Every write changed an indexed column, defeating HOT; reconciliation touched 43k buffers for 100 rows. |
| Spike task under `lib/` | `bench/`, compiled in dev only | Benchee must not ship; the task must not compile in test or prod. |
| Role and overwrite limits at Discord's caps (250 / 100 / 50) | 50 / 50 / 20, operator-configurable | Discord's caps are sized for communities of hundreds of thousands. |
| Credo AliasUsage at stock | Fires above two uses, excludes `test/support` | Generated test scaffolding should stay stock. |
| Gateway encoding negotiated by WebSocket subprotocol | Chosen by the opcode of the first frame (binary = protobuf, text = JSON) | Phoenix does not expose the negotiated subprotocol to the socket; the opcode is unambiguous and needs no negotiation. |
| `Authorization` header on the gateway upgrade | `?access_token=` query parameter for all clients in v1 | Phoenix sockets cannot read arbitrary headers; a custom upgrade plug is deferred. |
| Untrusted parsing (image decoding) in a Port-driven worker OS process | Dirty-scheduled Rust NIFs with input bounds checked first; a Port worker only for C-linked crates and video | Rustler catches Rust panics and Rust rules out C's memory bugs, so the Port defended against failures that cannot happen. Memory exhaustion is defended by bounds, not by process isolation. |
| BLAKE3 via a Rustler NIF (architecture §3 names it) | The pure-Elixir `b3` package, behind `Lumia.Crypto` | Identity inputs are 32-byte keys and small bodies; `b3` is maintained with no dependencies, while the `blake3` NIF package pins Rustler 0.30. Keeps the Rust toolchain out of contributor setup until MLS needs it. The facade makes a NIF for large blobs a one-line swap. |
| Overwrite with a bit in both allow and deny "treated as deny" at resolve time | Normalized on write: a denied bit is cleared from allow | The spec's own same-layer rule made allow win; property tests exposed the contradiction. Normalizing storage keeps the algorithm as written. |
| Each context broadcast as soon as its own `Repo.transact` returned | `Lumia.Tx.transact` plus `Tx.after_commit`: broadcasts wait for the outermost commit | Ecto flattens nested transactions, so `add_member`, `remove_member`, overwrites, the viewer diff, purges and roster acceptance all broadcast before their transaction committed. A rollback after the broadcast reused the `seq` the client had already applied. Found in review; invisible under the SQL sandbox. |
| Gateway subscribe checked `VIEW_CHANNEL`; HTTP event reads checked `READ_HISTORY` | Both check `READ_HISTORY` (delivery §6) | A member denied `READ_HISTORY` could replay 1,000 events by subscribing with cursor 0. Live-only delivery for such a member is deferred until a client rule for the gap exists. |
| `nodeup` re-resolved every subscription of every session at once | `nodeup` marks subscriptions stale; each is re-resolved before its next delivery | The same guarantee (nothing delivered on a pre-partition view) without every session on the node hitting Postgres in the same instant. |
| A session process stopped itself after pushing `Disconnect` | It also closes the transport | Phoenix kept the WebSocket open and reported the channel exit as `phx_close`, which the serializer rendered as a second `Disconnect{SERVER_SHUTDOWN}` telling a revoked client to reconnect. |
| Rotated refresh token simply unknown | `previous_refresh_hash` kept on the session; presenting it revokes the session | Protocol §12 promised reuse detection; the code answered `UNAUTHENTICATED`. Rotation is now conditional on the presented hash so two refreshes racing on one token cannot both succeed. |
| Reuse of a rotated refresh token always revokes the session (protocol 12 as first written) | For 60 seconds after a rotation the retired token is answered with the same pair again; after that, reuse revokes. A rotation derives its tokens from the one presented, under the instance's key, so the pair can be said again without being kept (Ryan, 2026-11-12) | A rotation the instance made whose answer never arrived left the client holding a retired token, and its next refresh revoked a session nobody had stolen. The price is that minute of reuse detection: a stolen token replayed inside it gets the pair too, and the two holders are told apart at the next rotation instead. |
| Identity made by an explicit create, its phrase confirmed then; the root key never enters a browser; reauth by password, TOTP or passkey (identity spec as first written) | An account has an identity from sign-up: native apps create it silently and ask for the phrase later, keeping its entropy until three words are confirmed; the web confirms in the sign-up flow and holds root material only within one flow (create anywhere, restore on the flagship's origin); reauth by every sign-in method, and carried by a credentialed sign-in; start over for someone with neither device nor phrase (architecture 5.3, 5.4, 11.4; identity 4.1, 4.7, 7, 10; Ryan, 2026-11-14, #20) | Nobody outside the CLI could make keys (#20). Making them at first need would put setup in the middle of both sides of every first conversation and reauth in front of each; a PIN was weighed for the web and rejected, being about 20 bits that only an enclave's guess limit makes safe. |

## Step 2, part 2: delivery, accounts, authorization

Ordered. Each task names the spec section it implements and the tests that close it. Tests come from the spec's test list; a task is done when its tests pass in CI.

**Foundations** (done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `accounts` table with `instance_role`, `last_seq`, `last_seq_at` (second granularity, `fillfactor = 70`), and `instance_settings` with `recovery_generation` read into `persistent_term` at boot by `Lumia.Instance` (delivery §2, §8).
- [x] `account_events` and `account_event_payloads`, hash-partitioned by `account_id`; `Lumia.Accounts.append_event/3` with the same write-path shape as `Lumia.Events` (delivery §2).
- [x] `servers`, `server_members`, `roles`, `member_roles`, `channel_overwrites`, `audit_log` with `Lumia.Audit.record/4` (authorization §2, §9). `channels` gained `server_id`, `name`, `topic`, `position`.
- [x] Oban 2.24 with `Lumia.Workers.PurgeCommands` on a daily cron (delivery §9). A retry after purge surfaces as `CONFLICT`, never a duplicate message; tested.

**Authorization** (done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `Lumia.Authz.resolve/2` over bitfields, `test/support/authz_reference.ex` over `MapSet`s, property tests between them (authorization §3). `Lumia.Authz.Permission` maps names to proto enum bits.
- [x] `Lumia.Authz.Cache`: ETS keyed `(channel_id, acl_version)` with TTL sweep; `fetch/2` returns `:stale` when the channel has moved on (authorization §4).
- [x] `Lumia.Servers`: definition-level changes bump `acl_version` in-transaction; server-wide bumps enqueue `Lumia.Workers.AclChanged` to append `acl_changed` events in batches; channel-level bumps append it directly (authorization §6.1).
- [x] Member-level changes append `membership_added`, `membership_updated`, `membership_removed`, `channel_removed` to the account log and touch no `acl_version` (authorization §6.2).
- [x] `Lumia.Workers.ViewerDiff`, debounced 30 s per server, diffing against a `channel_viewers` table (authorization §6.3).
- [x] Management hierarchy, owner rules, `@everyone` undeletable, operator limits via `:limits` config (authorization §3.3, §10).
- [x] `Lumia.Audit.record/4` called in every change's transaction (authorization §9).
- [x] `Lumia.Events.append_system/3` for server-originated channel events.

**Wire types** (done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `protobuf` 0.17 with generated `Lumia.V1.*` modules in `lib/lumia/proto`, regenerated by `scripts/gen_proto.sh`. Generated code is committed.
- [x] Event and account-event payloads stored as serialized `Lumia.V1.Event` / `AccountEvent` bytes, so the stored bytes are the broadcast bytes (delivery §4). `Lumia.Events.append/4` takes a `Lumia.V1.Command` and an actor; `append_system/3` and `Accounts.append_event/3` take payload tuples. Both broadcast after commit on `channel:<id>` / `account:<id>`.
- [x] `Lumia.Proto` converts schemas and scalars (UUID bytes, timestamps) to wire structs.
- [x] Edit and delete materialize; delete redacts the create event's payload (delivery §10).

**Sessions** (done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `sessions` table and `Lumia.Auth`: opaque 15-minute access and 30-day single-use refresh tokens stored as SHA-256; `verify_access/1` checks the row on every call; `refresh/1` rotates both; `revoke/1` marks the row, broadcasts on `session:<id>` so a live socket closes, and appends `SessionRevoked` to the account log; `revoke_device/2` for the identity spec's revoke flow (architecture §6.3, protocol §12).

**Gateway** (first pass done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `LumiaWeb.Gateway.Serializer`: raw `ClientFrame` in, bare `ServerFrame` out, `Hello` as join, event bytes wrapped without re-encoding; encoding chosen by the first frame's opcode (delivery §5; tests: serializer unit tests).
- [x] `LumiaWeb.Gateway.SessionChannel`: subscription state, the four-line delivery rule, authoritative resolve on subscribe and on `acl_version` change, membership-driven unsubscribe (delivery §5; tests: N+2 before N+1 in order, duplicates dropped, kicked member receives nothing, acl change caught).
- [x] Encode-once broadcast from `Lumia.Events` and `Lumia.Accounts` after commit (delivery §4).
- [x] One replay loop for subscribe, live gaps and nudges; 1,000-event cap answers `SNAPSHOT_REQUIRED` (delivery §6; tests: cap, gap fill).
- [x] `LumiaWeb.Gateway.Reconciler`: 5 s tick with 1 s overlap, node-local head broadcasts for channels and accounts (delivery §7; test: nudge fills a suppressed broadcast).
- [x] Account-log subscription at join, `ResumeAccount`, live account events (delivery §5).
- [x] Session revocation closes the socket (test).
- [x] Heartbeat echo, `Welcome` with recovery generation and limits.
- [x] Slow-consumer disconnect: the transport mailbox past 1,000 frames sends `Disconnect{SLOW_CONSUMER}` and stops (delivery §5; test).
- [x] Bounded staleness: a subscription not re-resolved in 10 minutes is re-resolved before its next delivery; `nodeup` marks every subscription stale so each is re-resolved before its next delivery, without a burst of queries (delivery §5, authorization §5; tests).
- [x] Review fixes (2026-11-02): `Lumia.Tx` defers every broadcast to the outermost commit; subscribe takes `READ_HISTORY` like the HTTP reads; the re-resolve branch runs once per event; `Disconnect` closes the transport; a second connection for a session closes the first with `PROTOCOL_ERROR`; malformed frames and ids answer `INVALID_ARGUMENT` instead of crashing the session; high-water marks are reported only for channels the account can read (tests).
- [x] Token on the upgrade (2026-11-02): `?access_token=` or, for browsers, the `base64url.bearer.phx.<token>` subprotocol in `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol` (Phoenix's `auth_token`); protocol §6.1 amended, since the socket layer cannot read `Authorization` and no client needs it to (test).
- Deferred to step 6, where the first UI defines their shape: presence scoped per server plus the friends topic (architecture §3; `PresenceUpdate` and `Typing` are accepted and ignored); the JSON overrides for bot clients (protocol §2; stock ProtoJSON for now).

**HTTP** (first pass done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `LumiaWeb.Plugs.ProtoBody` reads JSON or protobuf bodies; `LumiaWeb.ProtoConn` decodes into `Lumia.V1` structs and renders JSON or protobuf by `Accept`; `FallbackController` maps error reasons to the envelope and status table (protocol §2, §10).
- [x] `LumiaWeb.Plugs.Auth`: bearer access token verified against the session row on every request (protocol §12).
- [x] `GET /instance` (trust card), `POST /accounts` with Argon2id password, `POST /auth/sign-in/password`, `POST /auth/refresh`, `POST /auth/sign-out`, `GET /sessions`, `DELETE /sessions/{id}`.
- [x] `GET /account/snapshot`, `GET /account/events` (protocol §7).
- [x] `GET /channels/{id}/events`, `/snapshot`, `/messages` with `has_more`, `POST /channels/{id}/commands` with idempotent replay (delivery §8; test: snapshot and scrollback paging).
- [x] Servers, channels, members, roles, overwrites endpoints with authoritative checks through `Lumia.Authz.Access`, shared with the gateway; non-members get `NOT_FOUND` (authorization §5).
- [x] Malformed `*_id` path parameters answer `NOT_FOUND` through `LumiaWeb.Plugs.ValidateIds` instead of raising (found by the first CLI run).
- [x] Pagination (2026-11-02): members page by keyset over `(joined_at, account_id)` with an opaque cursor and `page_size` (protocol §11; test). Search lands with search.
- [x] `truncated_before` stays false by design: event rows are permanent and retention nulls payloads into `Redacted` rather than dropping rows, so no HTTP fetch is ever truncated. Revisit only if rows are ever deleted.
- [x] `Lumia.Workers.Retention` (daily): per-server `retention_days` set by `Servers.set_retention/3` (MANAGE_SERVER, audited); `Events.purge_before/2` nulls payloads, deletes messages, appends `PayloadsPurged`; resumed clients see `Redacted` (delivery §10; tests: purge, idempotence, no policy untouched).
- [x] TOTP as a second factor (2026-11-02): `POST /auth/totp/enroll` and `/confirm`, password sign-in answers `FAILED_PRECONDITION{mfa: "totp"}` until the code is given, reauth accepts `totp_code` (architecture §6.3, identity §10; test).
- Deferred to step 6 (they need a browser or a platform authenticator, or a UI to be shaped against): magic link, passkeys, OIDC (architecture §6.3); invites and discovery (architecture §6.6).

**Identity on the server** (first pass done 2026-11-01)

- [x] `Lumia.Crypto` facade: Ed25519 from `:crypto`, BLAKE3 from `b3`, HMAC.
- [x] Challenges: HMAC-bound, host-bound, two-minute expiry, `used_challenges` single use across nodes (identity §9; test: replay).
- [x] `Lumia.Identity.accept_roster/2` with the revocation policy: device-signed revocations only via the revoke endpoint with reauth, root-signed bypass; on acceptance revoked devices lose their sessions and `RosterUpdated` goes on the account log (identity §4.4; tests: refused on publish, accepted with reauth, root path).
- [x] `POST /auth/reauth` with password; tokens single use, five minutes (identity §10).
- [x] Link bundles: 8-character code, single use, ten minutes, five wrong attempts delete, 64 KB cap (identity §6; tests).
- [x] Identity backup put and get, highest version wins (identity §8).

## Step 3: Rust core

**Milestone 1** (2026-11-01): the sync engine talks to the real gateway.

- [x] Workspace at `apps/core`: `lumia-proto`, `lumia-core`, `lumia-client`, `lumia-ffi`, `lumia-wasm`; Rust 1.96 pinned; Apache-2.0; `AGENTS.md`; `core.yml` CI with fmt, clippy `-D warnings`, tests, wasm32 check.
- [x] `lumia-proto`: prost through protox from `proto/` at build time, nothing committed.
- [x] `lumia-core::sync::Session`: sans-I/O state machine with scripted tests for in-order apply, duplicates, gap fetch with a single in-flight fetch, snapshot path holding live events, nudges and high-water marks, generation change discarding cursors, subscription errors (protocol §5, delivery §8).
- [x] `Store` and `Keystore` contracts; `wire` scalar helpers.
- [x] `lumia-client`: SQLite store applying events, materialized messages and cursor in one transaction, refusing gaps; snapshot replace; outbox; HTTP client with protobuf bodies; gateway loop carrying out effects with heartbeat and outbox retry on welcome; file keystore (dev only).
- [x] `lumia-cli`: `instance add`, `account create`, `login`, `servers`, `server create`, `channels`, `channel create`, `send`, `tail`.
- [x] Reconnect with backoff: `gateway::run_forever` wraps one connection with exponential backoff (1s doubling to 30s, a quarter jitter), re-subscribes from the store's cursors, honours the server's `reconnect_after_seconds`, resets after a completed handshake, and stops on `SESSION_REVOKED`, `DEVICE_REVOKED`, `PROTOCOL_ERROR`, or when the application drops its sender. `tail` and `send` run on it.
- [x] Refresh-token rotation: the token pair lives behind a shared handle in `Api`; an `UNAUTHENTICATED` or `TOKEN_EXPIRED` answer with a refresh token on hand rotates once and retries the call, a 401 at the socket upgrade rotates before the next attempt, and a hook persists each new pair (the CLI and the FFI client write it to the keystore).
- [x] `lumia-cli scenario <name|all>`: the failure-injection cases a client can drive on its own against a running instance, each with its own throwaway server, channel, or identity: `token-rotation`, `frame-before-hello`, `duplicate-command`, `snapshot-under-writes`, `snapshot-threshold` (1,000 behind replays, 1,001 takes the snapshot), `stale-roster`, `device-revoke`, `reconnect`. All eight pass on 2026-11-01. Two server bugs found by them and fixed: a frame before `Hello` crashed the transport instead of answering `DISCONNECT_PROTOCOL_ERROR` and closing; a concurrent identical command that lost the race on the message row got `CONFLICT` instead of the winner's ack. The cases that need the server to misbehave (out-of-order broadcast, purged command row, generation bump, slow consumer, partition) stay server-side tests in `apps/instance`.

**Milestone 2** (2026-11-01): identity in the core.

- [x] `lumia-core::identity::phrase`: 24-word BIP39 phrase, HKDF derivation of the root and backup keys, identity id (identity §2).
- [x] `identity::keys`: Ed25519, XChaCha20-Poly1305, BLAKE3, root and device `Signature` helpers.
- [x] `identity::roster`: the eight-step verifier as a port of `Lumia.Identity.Roster`, builders for device entries, revocations, rotations and signed rosters (identity §3, §4).
- [x] `identity::link`: `LinkRequester` and `seal_bundle` over X25519 and HKDF (identity §4.2, §6).
- [x] Cross-implementation vectors: `apps/instance/test/lumia/identity/vectors_test.exs` writes `proto/vectors/identity/rosters.json` with `LUMIA_WRITE_VECTORS=1` and checks the Elixir verifier against it; `apps/core/crates/lumia-core/tests/identity_vectors.rs` checks the Rust verifier against the same file. 17 vectors, both agree.
- [x] `identity::proof` builds a proof from a host's challenge, refusing another host or purpose.
- [x] `lumia-cli`: `identity create` (phrase shown once, roster v1), `account create-by-identity`, `login --identity`, `identity link-request` / `approve` / `claim` (the full 4.2 flow with the bundle over X25519), `identity revoke --phrase` (root-signed, bypasses reauth), `identity show`.
- [x] Run end to end against the instance on 2026-11-01: create, account by proof, login by proof, link a second device, second device logs in, root revocation from the phrase, revoked device's session answers `SESSION_REVOKED` and its re-login is refused.
- [x] Older roster (identity §5): the instance now returns the held roster in `CONFLICT`'s `details.held` as protocol §9 said (it did not before). Sign-in, account creation by proof, and link approval adopt it, verifying it as a successor of the one held locally, and retry once; a device that is revoked in it gets `DeviceRevoked` and `identity show` marks it. Covered by the `stale-roster` scenario and the Elixir API test.
- [x] Device-signed revocation under reauth: `identity revoke <id> --password` reauths at `POST /auth/reauth`, signs the revocation with this device's key, and sends it to `POST /identity/devices/revoke`; `identity link-account --password` binds a password account to the identity at `POST /identity/link` under the same reauth. Covered by the `device-revoke` scenario, including the wrong-password refusal.

**Milestone 3** (2026-11-01): the binding surfaces, marshalling only.

- [x] `lumia-ffi` (UniFFI 0.32, proc macros): one `Client` object per instance with blocking methods for accounts, sign-in by password or identity, servers and channels, the local store's messages, the gateway loop (`start` with a `Listener` callback interface, `subscribe`, `send_message`, `foreground`, `stop`), and every identity flow the CLI has. A `ClientEvent` enum mirrors the gateway loop's events. The loop runs on the client's own tokio runtime and reports from a runtime thread. Uses the development store and keystore until the shells bring platform ones (steps 6 and 7); says so in its docs. Offline tests cover opening, identity creation, and the lifecycle guards.
- [x] `lumia-wasm` (wasm-bindgen): the sans-I/O `Session` as a JS class. Frames in as bytes, effects out as plain objects with a `kind` (`send`, `fetch`, `fetchAccount`, `snapshot`, `accountSnapshot`, `apply`, `applyAccount`, `discardAll`, `notify`), ids as `Uint8Array`, sequence numbers as numbers. The store and the socket are JS's, which is what step 6's SQLite-on-OPFS worker wants: the JS loop carries out `apply` with the cursor in one transaction and calls back `fetched` and `snapshotApplied`. Checked on `wasm32-unknown-unknown`.
- [x] Binding generation and a smoke from each language came with the shells (steps 6 and 7, by 2026-11-04): `wasm-bindgen` through `apps/web/scripts/wasm.ts` with the browser suite, `uniffi-bindgen` through `apps/apple/scripts/core.sh` and `apps/android/scripts/core.sh` with the macOS, iOS and Android smokes.
- [x] Review fixes (2026-11-02): the gateway loop carried out effects as a stack, so a fetched page of two or more events was applied newest-first and refused by the store; it is a FIFO queue now. The account snapshot path never moved the store's account cursor, so an account more than 1,000 events behind reconnected forever; `Store::apply_account_snapshot` sets it. A refused socket upgrade (Phoenix answers 403 for an expired token and a revoked session alike) was taken as `DEVICE_REVOKED` and ended the loop; it now refreshes and retries, and the refresh's answer decides. `discard_all` also clears the DM engine's per-channel progress. Refused commands leave the outbox (protocol §6.5). A short fetch page is followed by another fetch while a gap remains; the upper bound becomes the request's `limit`, which is what the instance reads. The heartbeat interval comes from `Welcome`. `apply` reads the cursor inside its transaction. `identity create` refuses a device that already has one.
- [x] `scenario roster-rules` (2026-11-02): hand-built rosters against the instance's verifier for the identity §12 cases a client can drive alone: a certificate signed by a device revoked before its `added_at` (refused at certs/signer), one signed before the revocation (accepted), a future-dated certificate (certs/future_dated), a roster dropping a held revocation (monotonic/revocation_dropped), and a root rotation after which the device's old certificate still verifies and it signs in under the new root.

## Step 4: identity across two instances

**First pass** (2026-11-01), two dev instances on one machine rather than compose: `config/dev.exs` reads `PORT`, `LUMIA_HOST` and `SECRET_KEY_BASE`, so a second instance runs as `PORT=4001 LUMIA_HOST=127.0.0.1 SECRET_KEY_BASE=… DB_URL=…/lumia_dev2 mix phx.server` beside the first. The two differ in host name (challenges are host-bound, identity §9), challenge key and database, which is what the identity flows care about.

- [x] The CLI keeps identity state in `<data>/identity/` (phrase-derived id, device key, roster), shared by every instance under that data directory; per-instance state (`base_url`, session, store) stays in `<data>/<host>/`. One device, many instances, as identity §5.1 says.
- [x] `identity status`: this device's roster version and the version each known instance holds, flagging an instance that is behind. `identity sync`: publish the held roster to every instance. `identity revoke --all`: revoke, then publish everywhere.
- [x] Run end to end: identity created on device A; an account bound to it on each instance; device B linked through instance 1 (instance 2 shows `v1 (behind)`); B signs in on both instances and instance 2 learns v2 from the proof's carried roster; A revokes B through instance 1 only (status shows instance 2 `v2 (behind)`, B's session on instance 1 answers `SESSION_REVOKED`, instance 2 still serves B); `identity sync` publishes v3 to instance 2 and B's session there answers `SESSION_REVOKED` too. The "revoked on 1 of 2" state from identity §5 is what `status` reports between the revoke and the sync.
- [x] From identity §12, run end to end with the Rust core as the client: revoke while another instance is behind, then reconcile (the first case, with the root-signed path across two instances; the device-signed path under reauth runs against one instance in the `device-revoke` scenario).
- [x] From identity §12, driven end to end on 2026-11-02 by `scenario roster-rules`: signer revoked before and after `added_at`, future-dated certificate, omitted revocation, root rotation. Phrase recovery with every device gone and the MLS rejoin ran in step 5.
- [x] Closed by step 8 on 2026-11-05: concurrent forks and the merge (the merge rule in the core, the two-instance script), proof replay across nodes and web-origin proofs (the named failure-injection suite).
- [x] Two instances in compose: `apps/instance/deploy/compose.two.yml` and `two-instances.sh` (step 8, 2026-11-05).
- [x] Phrase recovery with every device gone (2026-11-02, `identity recover --phrase`), against one instance; the second instance follows through `identity sync`.

## Step 5: MLS personal DMs, backup, recovery

- [x] OpenMLS 0.9.2 linked in a spike (2026-11-01): a two-member group with a welcome and an application message round-trips on Rust 1.96, and the crate compiles for `wasm32-unknown-unknown` with its `js` feature plus the `js`/`wasm_js` features of the three transitive getrandom majors. Its provider is crypto, randomness, and a 72-method typed key-value storage trait; WebCrypto has no HPKE, so the web proof obligation in architecture §20 resolved to keys in WASM memory with the OPFS store encrypted at rest.
- [x] `docs/specs/e2ee.md` written: suite 0x0001, basic credential of identity id and device id signed by the device key, leaf validation against the roster, key-package cadence (20, top up under 10, 90 days, one last-resort), group lifecycle (accepter creates; any device commits; epoch plus one at the delivery service; link approver adds leaves; revocation removes them), `DmOperation` inside the ciphertext with projection rules, client-encrypted attachments, SQLite storage provider in the store's transaction, web key handling, history backup (chunks under per-chunk keys, manifests under the backup key, on the hosting instance, off by default), recovery by external commit. Architecture 11.1, 11.3 and 11.4 amended; `e2ee.proto` and the history endpoints added to the protocol.
- [x] Core (2026-11-02): `lumia_core::mls` with the OpenMLS `StorageProvider` over an `MlsKv` key-value contract (`KvStorage`, `Provider`), leaf identities and `RosterLookup` validation, key packages with the last-resort capability, `Conversation` (create, join by welcome, external join from GroupInfo, add, remove, two-phase commit merge/discard, encrypt, `handle` for commits/proposals/application messages with sender validation, GroupInfo export, delete), and the `Projection`. Ten unit tests cover the three-device round trip, immediate refusal of a revoked leaf with one removal commit and a no-op retry, an unrostered key package, held future-epoch messages, external join, and reload from storage. Compiles for wasm32 with the getrandom JS features declared as target-specific dependencies.
- [x] Instance (2026-11-02): `Lumia.Dms` (`POST /dms/personal`, `dm_participants`, participant-gated access to `PERSONAL_*` channels), `Lumia.Mls` (key-package pool with 90-day expiry, one last-resort package served when the pool is empty and never deleted, claims under `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, newest GroupInfo by epoch, purge on revocation and expiry), the epoch rule in `Events.append` (`CONTROL` at current + 1 advances, at current is a welcome or proposal, else `FAILED_PRECONDITION{state: "epoch"}` with no seq taken), `Welcome.key_package_count`, `GET /accounts/{id}/roster` across a shared DM, `Lumia.History` (chunks and manifests as opaque bytes, monotonic manifest versions, quota with `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` 507), encrypted snapshots. 162 tests.
- [x] Client (2026-11-02): `SqliteStore` implements `MlsKv` and runs the engine's work in `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transactions so MLS state, the decrypted log, the processed cursor and the outbox entry commit together; `lumia_client::dm::DmEngine` (top-up on connect, rosters fetched per participant and re-fetched on a rejected leaf, group creation by the opener with claims for every device of both identities, commit then welcome then GroupInfo, own events merged or recorded when they come back through the log, removal commits on roster change, catch-up from the stored log so a restart never skips an event); `lumia-cli dm setup|open|tail|send|messages`. Run live against the dev instance with two identities and three devices: the opener creates the group with three leaves, both of the other identity's devices join from the welcome, messages go both ways and the three projections agree, a root-signed revocation makes the first device to learn of it remove the leaf in one commit, the other participant sees the removal, and the revoked device is refused at the gateway.
- [x] Second pass (2026-11-02): `RosterUpdated` on every accepted version and `ContactRosterUpdated` to every DM contact of the identity; the engine reconciles each conversation's leaves against the rosters on connect and on either signal, removing revoked devices and adding rostered devices with no leaf by claiming their key packages one device at a time; `identity recover --phrase` (identity spec 4.3: a provisional roster provokes `CONFLICT`, the held version comes back, every held device is revoked by the root and this one added, then sign-in); `dm rejoin` by external commit from the published GroupInfo, tolerating the identity's own revoked leaves in the tree and removing them in the commit that follows; history backup from the client (`dm backup on|off|status`, chunks under per-chunk keys written when a run goes idle, a device-signed manifest with a monotonic version, `dm restore` verifying each manifest against the roster, ignoring one signed by a device revoked before its `written_at`, unwrapping under the backup key and replaying with deduplication by operation id); a join epoch per conversation so a message encrypted before this device joined reads as history rather than a failure. Run live: a third device linked after the group existed was added by the approver on its next connect with one commit and read later messages while marking earlier ones as before its join; it restored those from the first device's backup; with every device gone, a fresh device recovered from the phrase, rejoined by external commit, removed the two revoked leaves in the next commit, read a message sent after that, and restored history with no duplicate operation; the other participant saw the recovered-device marker and the removals; the revoked devices were refused at the gateway.
- [x] Closing pass (2026-11-02): DM requests (`POST /dm-requests`, `/respond`; `DmRequestReceived`, `DmRequestUpdated`, pending requests in the account snapshot) with the accepter-creates rule: `POST /dms/personal` is gated by an accepted request or an existing personal channel, acceptance opens the channel, and `dm respond <id> accept` creates the group; personal group DMs (three or more participants, the creator creates the group); the identity backup (`identity backup`, written by `identity sync` to every instance; `identity recover` reads it after signing in and lists the hosts to sign in to); `dm delete` with the covering backup chunk rewritten without the message and the old chunk deleted; `scenario last-resort` (one package claimed and gone, the last-resort package served twice and kept, replaced by the next publish). Run live: refused open, request, accept with a two-leaf group, messages, a deletion whose chunk is rewritten so a third device restored everything but the deleted message, a group DM of four leaves read by all, and a phrase recovery that read the backup's host list.
- [x] Closed by step 8 on 2026-11-05: root rotation with the re-wrap (`identity rotate`), and the e2ee §12 fault cases in `apps/core/scripts/dm-faults.sh` (the crash between persist and send, the epoch moving under an unacked send, two members committing at once). The web device reload is step 6's.

## Step 6: web UI in Tauri

Settled in the step 6 grill (2026-11-02): SolidJS 1.9 with Vite, TanStack Solid Router, Solid Query and Solid Virtual; bun; Tauri 2; the core as WASM in Tauri first, in-process only when a measured budget misses on WebKitGTK; the Rust core owns the store on web through rusqlite on `sqlite-wasm-rs` in a dedicated worker, with Web Locks electing one owner tab and other tabs showing a take-over screen; the design system (tokens, fonts, 26 icons, logo set, 36 components) vendored from the design artifact once with the repo the master afterwards, shared assets under `assets/`; Playwright in Chromium and WebKit against the dev instance with the CLI as the other device. First screens: add an instance with the trust card, sign in by password or by linking this web device from a native device, Home with instances and personal DMs, a server's channel sidebar, a channel view with scrollback and a composer, personal DMs with requests, a device list with revoke. Out: voice, search, attachments, reactions UI, threads, settings beyond devices.

- [x] Spike (2026-11-02, [spikes/2026-11-02-web-store.md](spikes/2026-11-02-web-store.md)): the native store copied as is and `lumia-core` unchanged run in a Chromium worker on OPFS through rusqlite on `sqlite-wasm-rs`: 200 events applied in 15 ms, an MLS join and decrypt in 9 ms inside a store savepoint, and after a page reload the messages, cursor and group state come back from OPFS. Rust owns the store on web; the transport is ported the same way (reqwest's fetch backend, a web-sys socket, gloo timers), so Effect is not adopted. WebKit passes the same run, faster; WebKitGTK waits for the Linux CI build.
- [x] `lumia-client` builds for wasm32 (2026-11-02): the gateway loop runs over a per-target socket (`transport.rs`: tungstenite natively, gloo's WebSocket on the web) and timer (`time.rs`), on futures channels shared by native and wasm; the store opens on a named VFS and holds a keystore table for the web; the CLI and scenario suite moved to their own `lumia-cli` crate; CI checks `lumia-client` on wasm32 with the llvm archiver. On the web a refused upgrade has no visible status, so the loop refreshes the token before retrying.
- [x] `lumia-wasm` client surface (2026-11-02): a `Client` class per instance, opened in a worker on the OPFS pool VFS with the instance's store and keystore and the origin's identity stores (`lumia-<host>-*.sqlite`, `lumia-identity-*.sqlite`): the trust card, password sign-in with a second factor, account creation, the account snapshot and a channel's messages from the store, the gateway loop as a worker task with its events delivered to one listener as plain objects, sends through the loop's outbox, and the web-device link flow (request, claim, sign-in by identity proof). The bare `Session` surface stays for the sans-I/O tests. `apps/web/src/client/` is the bridge: the worker answers RPC calls and forwards events, the page keeps queries that the events invalidate.
- [x] `assets/` and `apps/web` (2026-11-02): the logo set, the 26 icons, both font families with licences and `design/tokens.json` vendored from the design artifact under `assets/`, which the web build serves as its public root; `apps/web` on SolidJS 1.9, Vite and bun with TanStack Solid Router, Query and Virtual installed; the 36 components ported to Solid with their stylesheet, `tokens.css` generated from the tokens on every build, and a `/design` gallery that rebuilds the Quiet Split and sign-in boards from the components, checked in both themes by screenshot; `web.yml` builds it in CI.
- [x] Web device linking (2026-11-02): the browser's device key is created in the origin's identity keystore on first open, the link request names `DeviceKind.Web` and the page's origin, the CLI approves and the browser claims and signs in by identity proof; both browsers end up in the roster as web devices. The instance trusts its own origin and the configured `web_origins` for the certificate check and now also for CORS (`LumiaWeb.Plugs.Cors`: a preflight from any other origin is refused), with `http://localhost:5173` configured in dev and test. Still open: the store encrypted at rest under a WebCrypto key.
- [x] First screens (2026-11-02): sign in (instance address, trust card, password with a second factor when asked, account creation when registration is open, the link button when identity-proof sign-in is enabled), link this browser, the take-over screen when another tab holds the store, and the Quiet Split with the server rail, the channel list, the channel view and the composer over the snapshot and messages queries; a reload reopens the saved instance and lands back in the channel. Still to come from the canvas: Home with instances and personal DMs, the DM screens, the device list.
- [x] Browser suite (2026-11-02): `bun run e2e` in `apps/web` seeds a dev instance with the CLI and, in Chromium and WebKit, signs in by password, sends a message and reads it back after a reload, then links the browser from the CLI and signs in by identity proof; about one second per flow per browser.
- [x] DM engine in the worker (2026-11-02): `lumia-wasm`'s `Client` builds the engine from the device's identity on its own store connection, feeds it every gateway event (Welcome tops up key packages; account events subscribe personal DMs once their participants are known), carries its outcomes (commands to the loop, `dm*` events to the page), and exposes open, request, respond (the accepter creates the group), send, delete, rejoin and the projected conversation; plus members lookup and device revoke with the password as reauth. The engine sits behind an async mutex so the gateway task and the page's calls take turns.
- [x] Artboards for Home, requests and devices (2026-11-02): not on the design canvas, so built as gallery boards from the components (four new icons; notice, request card, device list and empty state added to the system) and shown for approval before wiring: `apps/web` `/design`, boards "Home", "Requests", "Devices".
- [x] Home, requests, conversation, new message and devices wired to the engine (2026-11-02, boards approved as drawn): `/app?place=home` with the DM list grouped by host (the account snapshot now lists the personal DMs the account is in, with participants), the encrypted conversation over the engine's projection with notices for what this device cannot read, requests with accept (the browser creates the group), decline and block, starting a conversation with someone from a shared server (a request when the gate is closed), and the device list with revoke by password. The browser suite's DM case: the CLI asks, the browser accepts and creates the group, both send, the browser reloads and reads both (e2ee §12 web case), in Chromium and WebKit.
- [x] Web binary size (2026-11-02): a `web` Cargo profile (opt-level z, fat LTO, one codegen unit, abort on panic) and `wasm-opt -Oz` after wasm-bindgen (binaryen from npm, so CI has it) take the binary from 7.0 MB to 3.0 MB, about 1.0 MB brotli-compressed over the wire; the suite passes on it. The next cut would be a lighter SQLite build (fewer `SQLITE_ENABLE_*`), which `sqlite-wasm-rs` fixes in its own flags.
- [x] The browser suite in CI (2026-11-02): `e2e.yml` runs a Postgres service, the dev instance, the release CLI and Chromium on every push to the web, core or instance; the first green run took four minutes for the suite step and about seven in all with the Rust and browser caches warm. WebKit's coverage is the macOS run (step 7 notes why).
- [x] Public profiles (2026-11-02): `GET /accounts/{id}` returns another account's handle, display name and avatar when the caller is it, shares a server or a personal DM with it, or has a request waiting between them; the page resolves DM participants and request senders through it, fetched once each. The browser suite checks the CLI's handle on the request card and as the reply's sender.
- [x] The gateway token off the URL (2026-11-02): both transports send it as the `base64url.bearer.phx.` subprotocol beside `phoenix` (gloo's multi-protocol opener on the web, the upgrade request's header natively; the scenario suite's raw sockets use the same builder), and the server reads only that; the query form is gone from the protocol. Found on the way: Phoenix takes `auth_token` as a socket option and silently overwrote the one inside `websocket:`, so the header path had never worked; it is a socket option now.
- [x] Checked against a server that refuses every upgrade (2026-11-02): the two scenarios that use the gateway fail as they should (`frame-before-hello` with the 403, `snapshot-threshold` with `UNAUTHENTICATED`); the other eight exercise HTTP endpoints only and pass by design. A single-scenario run printed nothing on success, which is what made the earlier report unreadable; it now prints the same `== name` and verdict as `all`.
- [x] The OPFS stores encrypted at rest (2026-11-02): `lumia-wasm` links SQLite3 Multiple Ciphers (the `sqlite3mc` build of `sqlite-wasm-rs`, ChaCha20 only) and opens every store on the cipher shim over the OPFS pool VFS with a raw 32-byte store key; the worker keeps that key wrapped by a non-extractable AES-GCM WebCrypto key per origin in IndexedDB (e2ee §8 amended to say so). The browser suite checks that no OPFS file carries SQLite's plaintext header. The web binary grew from 3.2 to 7.0 MB before the size pass below.
- [x] `apps/desktop` (2026-11-02): a Tauri 2 shell (its own Cargo workspace, `core:default` permissions only) around `apps/web`'s build, the core as WASM inside it as in the browser. The page tells the worker it runs in the shell, which makes the device NATIVE with no origin (identity spec); the instance accepts Tauri's fixed local origins for CORS and for the gateway's `check_origin` (now the same rule, in `LumiaWeb.Plugs.Cors`), never as web-device origins. Built on macOS: a 10 MB app and a 5 MB disk image that open to the sign-in screen. `desktop.yml` lints the crate and builds the bundles on Linux (WebKitGTK 4.1) and Windows, uploading them as artifacts; its first run is on this push. In-process core waits on a measured WebKitGTK budget (architecture §13).
- [x] The instance serves the web UI (2026-11-02): `bun run build:instance` in `apps/web` builds in instance mode into `apps/instance/priv/static/ui` (not in the repo); the endpoint serves its hashed and shared assets immutably and the router's last route renders `index.html` for every path the API does not claim (`LumiaWeb.UiController`; an unknown `/api` path stays a JSON 404; without a build the instance says so). The served build takes the page's own origin as the instance and asks for no address, and the instance's own web origin is now its endpoint URL, so the web-device certificate check and CORS match what it serves. The browser suite runs against a served UI with `LUMIA_BASE`; all six flows pass that way too.
- [x] The web app follows the system's light or dark setting (2026-11-12, from Ryan using the web app, which stayed light on a dark Mac). The page had named a theme, `data-theme="light"` on its root, and nothing ever changed it outside the gallery. The page names none now: `tokens.css` carries the dark colours under `prefers-color-scheme` for a root with no `data-theme` (and `color-scheme`, so the browser's own scrollbars and controls follow), the logo's light and dark files swap the same way, and the change is live as the system's setting changes. The gallery still names one, to show a board in either, takes the system's the first time, and takes the name off the root when it goes away. Checked in the suite: the ground is `bg`'s light value under a light system and its dark one under a dark one.
- [x] The web app is a frame the size of the window (2026-11-12, from the same report: the whole page scrolled where the messages should have). The shell had the board's wrapping row, and a wrapping row takes its height from its tallest item, so a channel longer than the window made the page that long. `AppShell` is a row that does not wrap, as tall as the window and no taller (`100dvh`): the rail's servers, the sidebar's list, the message list, a page's body and the aside's list each scroll in themselves. The board's widths stand (the rail and the sidebar 304 together, the aside's column 300), where the wrapping row had let all three grow. Below 1100 wide the aside leaves the row and comes over the conversation's end from a Members button in the header, over a scrim; below 720 the frame is folded as the iOS boards have it, the rail and the sidebar as one screen and what is being read as a panel over them with the boards' back button and 44-high rows. Checked in the suite (forty messages sent: the page is no taller than the window and the list overflows its pane) and drawn headlessly at 1440 by 900, 900 by 700 and 390 by 844. **Not seen in a browser's own window by anyone**, and not on a phone: Safari on iOS, where the viewport moves with the keyboard, is unchecked.
- [x] The web app against the boards, by eye (2026-11-12, as the desktop shell's second pass in step 9, and to the same decisions). The app was drawn in Chromium at 1440 by 900 beside the Quiet Split, Profile card and Sign in boards, light and dark. What changed: the server's name is the board's button with its chevron and opens a menu (Invite people, New channel), each a popover in the profile card's vocabulary, with the board's New message pencil leading to Home's new message, where blue links had hung at the sidebar's foot; the rail's self avatar opens the board's account menu (who is signed in, the status as three either-or rows that take effect at once, View profile, Devices, Sign out), its dot the status chosen while the gateway is connected and a quiet one while it is not, with the connection in its hint; **the web app can sign out**, which it could not before (the session, the gateway loop, the queries and this browser's push subscription go; the stores stay, as on every client); a member's row and a message's author open the profile card (the band in the person's tone, the avatar over its edge, name, handle, presence in the server on screen, what the verified profile says, and Message, which opens a conversation or asks for one, or sends a browser with no device key to link itself); the side panel has the board's "Find a member" and, having one tab, the tab's words as its heading, with no "View all" button over a list that is already all of it and no "Members" group repeated under the heading when the instance reports no presence; one person's messages within five minutes are one block under one name, the later ones with their time in the avatar's column under the pointer, blocks 8 apart (`byBlock`, the desktop's `joins`), in channels and personal conversations, with day dividers in the latter where one "Messages" rule had stood; who is typing is named under the composer and this browser says when its person types (protocol 6.7; the wasm client already carried both); sign-in is the board's page (the card's 18 between its parts, the "or" rule after the primary button, every other way in as an outlined button with its glyph, the trust card as an inset block with "Change instance" at its end, "New to Lumia?" under the card, and the instance's Privacy and Terms at the foot where it names them), shared by the link, invite and other-tab screens; a page's cards and rows keep a card's measure instead of running the width of the window; what cannot be pressed is drawn at half strength; an empty channel says so in the middle of its pane. **What the boards draw with nothing behind it is no longer drawn in the app**, as on the desktop: the sidebar's search, the header's call, pins, search and settings, and the composer's attach, formatting, emoji and mention buttons had all been there and done nothing. The gallery still draws them, and gained the new pieces (Pieces board). The design system gained two tokens the boards use and had no name for (`shadow-floating` for what opens over the frame, `scrim` for the veil behind a sheet) and two icons (`back` from the iOS boards, `members`); the four menu and card icons the desktop had are in the web's table. Found on the way and fixed: an invite with no expiry read "expires Invalid Date" in the list (the field is absent, not null); a sidebar row or tile added a history entry on every press (they were links to `#`); the suite's handoff step failed whenever its token began with a dash (the CLI read it as an option); **a reload could land on sign-in with the session intact**: the worker of the page before still held the stores' OPFS access handles for a moment, the open failed and the route took that for a signed-out browser, which a channel with forty new messages made happen every time (the worker now waits the handles out, up to ten seconds); and in WebKit Escape did not close a menu after a press inside it, since Safari does not focus a button it is clicked on and the key went to the page's body (the popover listens on the document). The desktop shell's menus, popovers and profile card take `shadow-floating` too, where they had borrowed the action toolbar's lighter shadow for want of the token. Checked by the browser suite in Chromium and WebKit against an instance built from this tree, which now drives the menus, the card, the popovers, the theme, the scrolling and signing out; by the desktop's 154 tests; and drawn headlessly in both themes. Typing was seen once, drawn under the composer while the CLI typed as someone else; the suite does not cover it. Not checked: a screen reader on the menus and the card (they have roles, labels, arrow keys, Escape and a focus that stays inside and returns to what opened them, by reading the code and by the suite's role queries only).

The page takes the snapshot the call answers with, so the rail and the account menu follow at once. Checked by a step of the browser suite in Chromium and WebKit against an instance built from this tree: a rename, a taken handle, and "A b".
- [x] Pictures on the web (2026-11-12, after the profile photo in step 9). The Account card has the photo: the picture or the initials, "Choose a photo…" or "Change photo…", "Remove", and the desktop's hint and refusals; a file over 8 MB is refused before it is sent. Every avatar the app draws is the person's picture where they have one (the rail, the account menu, messages, member lists, conversation rows, requests, the profile card), with the initials where there is none or it will not load. A picture is an `<img>` straight from the instance, and only for a path of the instance's own shape (`/api/v1/avatars/` and 64 hexadecimal digits, the core's `is_own_avatar`), so nothing an instance says makes the page fetch from anywhere else. One thing in the core on the way: the wasm binding dropped the refusal's details, so every refused file read "could not be read"; it passes them now. The message list is not virtual (Solid Virtual is installed and unused) and reads the newest 200 with no way further back, where the desktop pages as the reader scrolls. The folded frame keeps what is open in a signal, so the browser's back button leaves the app instead of returning to the list. A channel's place in the address is only the server's (`?server=`), so a reload returns to the server's first channel. Personal conversations have no typing on any client (step 9).

## Step 7: multi-node, push, native shells

Settled in the step 7 grill (2026-11-02): the order is the deployment spec with multi-node, then the instance side of push, then the relay, then SwiftUI (macOS first, iOS on the same package), then Compose. Multi-node tests start a second BEAM node with OTP's `:peer` inside ExUnit, both on one Postgres, partitions by `Node.disconnect`; a two-container compose file is the operator's example only. Clustering stays on `dns_cluster` (one DNS name, one record per node, which Kubernetes, Fly and scaled Compose all give) rather than libcluster, whose extra strategies (multicast gossip, static Epmd lists) buy little here; architecture 15.1 amended. Blobs stay out until attachments exist; the deployment spec states the shared-store precondition. The spec covers profiles, the backup set (`bin/lumia backup`: `pg_dump`, the blob volume, a manifest naming the master key's location without copying it; `restore` checks the manifest and the blob references and bumps the recovery generation), expand-and-contract with N and N-1 coexisting, a Prometheus endpoint, and a multi-stage image that builds the web UI; OpenTelemetry and opt-in statistics later. Push: subscriptions per device, pushes for personal DMs and mentions to devices with no live session, content-free; Web Push with VAPID served by the instance, generic on web since the service worker cannot reach the store; UnifiedPush; the relay as `apps/relay` in Elixir with APNs and FCM behind an interface and a fake provider until credentials exist. Native: one Swift package (bindings, client, view models, tokens, small components) with separate layouts per app, macOS 14 and iOS 17, Android 9; Swift and Kotlin tokens generated from `assets/design/tokens.json`; UniFFI bindings generated at build and the core shipped as an XCFramework, never committed; the iOS screens get artboards shown first. Xcode 27 is on this machine.

- [x] `docs/specs/deployment.md` (2026-11-02) retires architecture 15: what an instance is (database, blobs, master key), the two profiles and their boot-time preconditions, every environment variable, the backup set and the two release commands with the recovery-generation bump on restore, encryption at rest by responsibility, expand-and-contract with N and N-1, observability, and the tests.
- [x] The four cross-node cases (2026-11-02) run on two real BEAM nodes: `Lumia.PeerNode` starts a peer with OTP's `:peer` over a TCP control channel (so calls cross a partition), hands it this node's configuration persistently with the repo on a plain pool, and the test module runs outside the sandbox and truncates after itself. A commit on the peer without its broadcast reaches a session here within one reconciler tick; a partition heals through reconciliation with nothing delivered twice; a removal here leaves the peer no resolution for the member and puts the removal on the member's log (the member-level path of the authorization spec, not an ACL bump, which the delivery spec's wording had implied); broadcasts from the peer out of order are delivered in order once.
- [x] Release commands (2026-11-02): `bin/lumia-admin migrate | backup <dir> | restore <dir> [--replace]` over `Lumia.Release`, each on a repo of its own for the target database (a release's `eval` has none running; the tests' is sandboxed). Backup is `pg_dump` in custom format from one serializable snapshot plus a manifest (schema version, recovery generation, host, time, blob store, the master key's SHA-256 fingerprint); restore checks the fingerprint, the schema version and emptiness (or empties the schema under `--replace`, since `pg_restore --clean` cannot drop the partitioned logs' inherited constraints), restores, migrates up and bumps the recovery generation. Tested end to end against a scratch database; CI installs the Postgres 18 client tools. `/metrics` serves Prometheus text when `LUMIA_METRICS` is on: request, route and query duration distributions, VM memory and run queues, and counters for gateway connects, acks, reconciler ticks and the heads found behind. Every setting of the spec's section 3 is read in `runtime.exs`.
- [x] The image and the compose example (2026-11-02): `apps/instance/Dockerfile` builds the web UI on the Rust image, the release on the Elixir image, and runs on Debian with the Postgres 18 client; `rel/env.sh.eex` names each node by its address with long names so DNS clustering works; `deploy/compose.yml` runs Postgres, the instance and Caddy in front (where TLS terminates), and `--scale instance=2` gives two nodes that see each other through Compose's DNS. Checked locally: the trust card and the served UI through the proxy, two clustered nodes, a backup into the mounted volume.
- [x] Push on the instance (2026-11-02): `push_subscriptions` per device and kind (`PUT`/`DELETE /push/subscription`), a VAPID key pair generated at first boot and published on the trust card, `LumiaWeb.Presence` tracking devices with a live session cluster-wide, the decision after each commit (a personal DM application message, or an account mention in a server message) carried out by an Oban job that pushes the generic notice to every offline device of the recipients and never to the actor, through `Lumia.Push.Sender`: Web Push with RFC 8291 `aes128gcm` and RFC 8292 VAPID written on `:crypto` alone (the RFC's test vector passes; the only Hex package used the older `aesgcm`, which Safari refuses), UnifiedPush as a plain POST, and the relay call for APNs and FCM tokens, which waits for `apps/relay`. On the web: a service worker shows the notice and opens the app, and the page subscribes with the instance's key after sign-in, best effort.
- [x] CI's browser suite runs Chromium on the Linux runner; WebKit runs on macOS (every local run, both browsers, all six flows, and in CI since the macOS job below). Playwright's Linux WebKit build passed the flows there too but hung on a profile's first navigation and on the conversation page's teardown often enough to block every run, and it is not the engine any user has.
- [x] The Linux runner's "renderer wedge" (2026-11-06): not the renderer, and not OPFS. Bounded probes added to the suite showed the page's main thread, its worker, a raw CDP evaluate and a screenshot all unanswered, and Chromium's own log (`DEBUG=pw:browser`) showed why: `Connection terminated while reading from pipe`, then a clean exit, a second after launch. Bun 1.3.x on Linux closes a finished child's extra stdio descriptors by number when its finalizer runs, and by then the numbers belong to the next browser's DevTools pipe ([oven-sh/bun#47956](https://github.com/oven-sh/bun/issues/47956)); Playwright never sees the pipe close, so every call hangs. The step with the most CLI children (the DM step) wedged first, then the invite step once more steps spawned more. The upstream recipe reproduces it three of three times under Bun 1.3.11 in a Linux container and zero of three under 1.4.4; the suite passes there under 1.4.4 with the DM reopen check back on Linux. Playwright's Linux WebKit hangs (the entry above) may have been the same bug; not retested, since no user runs that build. Bun is 1.4.4 in every workflow and in `.tool-versions`; the e2e job and the suite step have hard timeouts (a run once sat for the six-hour default after this), and the suite exits explicitly so a call that never settles cannot hold a job.
- [x] Web Push end to end in the browser suite (2026-11-05), with a push service of the suite's own, since a headless browser has no account with a real one: the suite's `Bun.serve` is the endpoint, the subscription's P-256 and auth keys are made in the test, and a `pushManager` stand-in injected before the page loads answers `subscribe` with them (the headless shell also denies every permission whatever the grant, so `Notification.permission` is the suite's too), after which everything is the app's own path: "Turn on notifications" registers the service worker and sends the subscription to the instance through the client. The browser then goes away, a member who joined through an invite mentions its account, the instance posts the notice encrypted for those keys (`aes128gcm`, RFC 8291) under its VAPID authorization (RFC 8292), and the test decrypts it with WebCrypto and expects the generic "New message". The instance accepts a plain-http endpoint on a loopback address only where `push_loopback_endpoints` is set (development and test; never production). The service worker's own `push` event cannot be raised from outside, so what it would show is what is checked. Chromium only: Playwright's WebKit has no service workers.
- [x] `apps/relay` (2026-11-02): a Plug application; `POST /push` forwards a content-free notice to APNs (HTTP/2, an ES256 provider token from the `.p8` key, a mutable visible alert) or FCM (HTTP v1, a service-account access token from an RS256 assertion, a data message) behind `Lumia.Relay.Provider`, with a fake provider for tests and credential-less runs; a dead token answers 410 so the instance forgets it. The requests are built by pure functions the tests check, including both signatures; the sends wait for credentials. `relay.yml` checks it in CI. Deployable since 2026-11-04: its own `Dockerfile` (the release alone) and `fly.toml` (one small machine, started by the first push after idling), the credentials inline (`APNS_KEY`, `FCM_SERVICE_ACCOUNT`) where a host's secrets are variables, and the instance's `fly.toml` naming it; the image was built and answered health and a push through the fake provider. What remains is operational: the APNs key with the push service in the developer portal, the secrets, `fly deploy` for both apps, and a message to a phone running the TestFlight build with the app closed.
- [x] The shared `App` (2026-11-02): `lumia_client::app::App` is the one shell-facing client (open with a store opener per place, sign in, snapshot, the gateway loop with an event sink, identity, push, DMs); `lumia-wasm` and `lumia-ffi` wrap it and convert types, nothing more, so a method lands once for every shell. The `uniffi-bindgen` crate pins the generator to the workspace's UniFFI.
- [x] `apps/apple` (2026-11-02): `LumiaKit` (the Swift package: the generated bindings as their own target, `LumiaClient` as an actor over them with the listener as an `AsyncStream`, `LumiaSession` as the observable the screens read, tokens generated from `tokens.json` with colours resolving per appearance, the small components) and `Lumia-macOS` (XcodeGen; sign-in with the trust card and the second factor, link, the Quiet Split with the server rail, a server's channels and messages, Home with conversations, requests, new message and devices) at parity with the web screens. `scripts/core.sh` builds the core, the bindings, the XCFramework and the tokens; nothing generated is committed. Checked: the package's tests, the app signing in against a dev instance, loading the snapshot and connecting to the gateway (`scripts/smoke.sh`; screenshots need screen-recording permission this terminal lacks). Fonts: the brand faces are WOFF2, which Apple's loader does not read, so the type styles map onto the system faces until TrueType copies are added to the assets. UniFFI's generator runs Xcode's `swift-format` on its output, which hangs on Xcode 27; `--no-format`.
- [x] TestFlight for the macOS app (2026-11-02): one App Store Connect record, `chat.lumia`, for macOS and iOS; Xcode registered the App ID and a cloud-managed distribution certificate through the account; `scripts/release.sh --upload` sent build 98 (universal, sandboxed). Export compliance is answered in App Store Connect on the first build; the code it issues goes in the plist as `ITSEncryptionExportComplianceCode`. Superseded as the channel by the GitHub releases below; kept for a store listing later.
- [x] Releases on GitHub with updates built in (2026-11-02; architecture section 13, "Distribution"): `.github/workflows/release.yml` runs on a `v*` tag only, drafts the release, builds the macOS DMG (`apps/apple/scripts/dist.sh`: ad-hoc-signed archive, Developer ID export with the certificate the workflow imports from a secret, since this account's API keys are not offered cloud signing, notarized and stapled, the Sparkle appcast with a delta against the previous release) and the Tauri bundles for Windows and Linux (`tauri-action`, with the signed updater artifacts and `latest.json`), then publishes the release so "latest" only ever names a complete one. The version is the tag; the macOS build number is the commit count. The macOS app carries Sparkle (a "Check for updates…" item and the daily check's switch in the application menu); the Tauri shell checks at launch and asks before installing, its switch waiting on the web UI's settings page and a bridge command. Checked locally: the Developer ID export signs and carries the sandbox entitlements, the appcast signs with the key, the Tauri build produces the updater archive and its signature. Run on GitHub (2026-11-03): `v0.1.0` is published with the DMG, the AppImage, deb, rpm, MSI and NSIS installers, their signatures, `appcast.xml` and `latest.json`, and both feeds resolve through the "latest" URL. Two things the first runs taught: a directory holding only generated files is absent on a fresh checkout (`core.sh` creates it), and an App Store Connect API key cannot use the team's cloud-managed Developer ID certificate, so the certificate is a `.p12` secret (`DEVELOPER_ID_P12`, its password) the job imports into a keychain of its own; the API key keeps notarization.
- [x] The macOS job (2026-11-03, `macos.yml`, on `macos-26`): one runner carries everything that needs a Mac, since its minutes are the dear ones. `core.sh --host` builds this runner's slice only (the universal library is the release's), then `LumiaKit`'s tests, the unsigned debug build of the macOS app, the dev instance on a Homebrew Postgres, `smoke.sh` against it, and the browser suite in WebKit on the same instance and web build. The core's C objects now take the apps' floor (`MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET`, `IPHONEOS_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET` set by `core.sh`), which ends the linker's newer-SDK warnings. First green run on 2026-11-03: 22 minutes with cold caches (the core, the CLI and the wasm build take four, five and three; the smoke 20 seconds; the three WebKit flows four and a half minutes), the app shows its window and connects, all three WebKit flows pass. The first run taught that the runner's Xcode 26 clang has no WebAssembly target, where Xcode 27's does, so the job compiles SQLite with Homebrew's LLVM (`CC_wasm32_unknown_unknown`).
- [~] iOS from artboards (shown first), then the notification extension with the single-writer lock. The artboards (2026-11-03): drawn first as a "Phone" board in `apps/web` `/design` and as sixteen frames (light and dark) on the design canvas beside the Quiet Split and sign-in boards, then settled on the canvas the same day. The Quiet Split folds as "rail and sidebar, floating panels": the rail and the sidebar stay one screen (Home: the title and New message, search, the Requests, Saved and Devices tiles, then conversations with their last line, time and unread count; a server: its name and member count, search, the Threads, Mentions and Saved tiles, channels, private channels and DMs), and the main pane and the aside become floating panels pushed over it with a back button, a title and subtitle, and the actions in a 64 px header (a conversation with the person's avatar, presence and a call button; a channel with members and settings; a thread with the parent, the replies and the reply field; requests in "Waiting for you" and "Sent" sections; devices with the revoke confirmation as a bottom sheet). There is no tab bar; the status bar and the home indicator are the system's. Sign in is the card with the instance as a row (its tile, host, operator and version, and Change), "Forgot password?", the link button with a phone glyph, and Help, Privacy and Terms below. The gallery board mirrors the canvas with the gallery's sample data.
- [x] `Lumia-iOS` (2026-11-03): the phone app on `LumiaKit`, laid out from those frames: the rail beside Home's conversations (each with its last line) or a server's channels as the root of a navigation stack, and a conversation, a channel, requests, a new message and devices (the revoke confirmation as a sheet) pushed as floating panels with their own header; sign in with the instance as a row and the link button; link this phone with copy and share. `AppStore` moved into the package so both apps share the reopen-at-launch and the debug sign-in. `core.sh --ios` adds the device and simulator slices; the simulator build is arm64. `scripts/smoke-ios.sh` installs the debug build in a simulator, launches it once per screen through `LUMIA_DEV_SCREEN` and screenshots each; run here against the dev instance, every screen as drawn, the gateway live (the CLI's message arrives in the channel view). The macOS CI job builds it and runs that smoke too. Known gap: with the system navigation bar hidden the edge-swipe back is off, so a panel's own back button is the way back until a custom gesture is added. The notification extension and the push subscription followed the same day (below).
