- [x] The notification extension with the single-writer lock (2026-11-03, architecture 15.5): `lumia-client`'s `writer.rs` is an advisory file lock beside an instance's stores (the standard library's, no new dependency); the app holds it shared in the foreground, `App::background` releases it, `App::resume` takes it again and reports whether another process wrote (a writer generation in the store's meta), in which case `LumiaSession` restarts the gateway loop from the store's cursors. `App::notification_content` is the extension's one call: under the exclusive lock (tried for a few seconds; the app holding it is live and shows the message itself) it brings every channel of the account to the instance's head over HTTP, applies the events, runs the DM engine's catch-up on personal channels, and returns the newest message for this account (a DM, or a server message that mentions it) with the sender's name. On the phone: the stores moved to the app group `group.chat.lumia`, the app registers with APNs and sends the token as the `apns` subscription, the scene phase drives the lock, and `Lumia-iOS/Notifications` is the service extension that rewrites the generic notice with that call. Run in the simulator (`scripts/smoke-push.sh`): the phone linked as a second device of a CLI identity through files, a DM accepted by the first CLI device so the group had the phone as a leaf, a message sent by the other identity, and with the app terminated the call joined from the welcome and decrypted the message with the sender's handle as the title. Two things the simulator taught: an unsigned build carries no app-group entitlement (the simulator build is signed to run locally now), and `simctl push` adds a notification directly without launching a service extension, so a debug launch stands in for the extension there and the extension's own launch is checked on a device. The relay payload names the instance by host, which the extension maps to a base URL the app recorded. A tap opens the conversation (2026-11-04): the app is the notification centre's delegate, the channel and kind the extension wrote into the content become a target the shell opens once the snapshot has the channel (a DM from Home, a mention in its server; the generic notice names none), and in the foreground the banner still shows. The push smoke ends with that tap now: a UI test holds the app, the rewritten push lands, the test taps the banner by what it says and finds the conversation's panel.
- [x] The iOS release (2026-11-03): `scripts/release.sh` is the iOS archive and App Store Connect upload now (the store is the only channel a phone has; `Lumia-iOS/ExportOptions.plist`, automatic signing, `core.sh --ios-device` so the archive builds two Rust targets, not four), and `release.yml` runs it on a `v*` tag in an `ios` job beside the desktop ones, signed through the API key, without gating the GitHub release. The macOS app leaves the store path: it is distributed as a direct download only; the `chat.lumia` record keeps its macOS platform (App Store Connect cannot remove one) with no version submitted and build 98 expired in TestFlight. The first upload went through on 2026-11-04 as build 127 of 0.2.1 (by `ios-upload.yml`, the on-demand run, after the tag's job failed twice): the runner's default Xcode 26.6 refuses the API key for automatic signing where 26.5 and 27 accept it, so the jobs name 26.5, and the iOS icon is now the brand symbol flattened on the dark ground (`scripts/icon.py`), as the store refuses an alpha channel.
- [x] Compose on the same core (2026-11-03): `apps/android` with `lumiakit` (the generated Kotlin bindings over `lumia-ffi` through JNA, `LumiaClient` as suspend calls on the IO dispatcher with the listener as a `Flow`, `LumiaSession` and `AppStore` as Compose state, the tokens generated into Kotlin, the components) and `app`, the phone layout folded as the canvas's boards and the iOS app lay it out: the rail beside Home's conversations or a server's channels, and the conversation, channel, requests, new message and devices screens pushed as floating panels, with the revoke confirmation as a bottom sheet. `scripts/core.sh` builds the core per ABI with `cargo-ndk` and generates the bindings and tokens; `scripts/emulator.sh` and `scripts/smoke.sh` boot a headless API 35 emulator, install the debug build, launch it once per screen through the intent's `LUMIA_DEV_*` extras and screenshot each. Run here: signed in, connected, every screen as drawn, the CLI's message arriving live. Two things the first run taught: UniFFI's Kotlin bindings cannot have an error variant with a field named `message` (it collides with `Exception.message`), so `LumiaError`'s is `detail` now; and Rust's standard file locking reports itself unsupported on Android, so the writer lock is a no-op where the platform says so (no second process shares the stores there). `android.yml` builds the arm64 core and the debug APK in CI; the emulator smoke stays local for now. Still to come: the Play Store build, and the debug APK's size (the debug core is a few hundred megabytes across two ABIs; the release build strips).
- [x] Push on Android (2026-11-04): UnifiedPush through any installed distributor (the connector's `PushService`; the endpoint as the `unified_push` subscription, the core's FFI taking that platform now) and FCM through the relay (the token as `fcm`; live only in a build with a Firebase project's `google-services.json`, which the repo does not carry), with the receivers sharing one path in `lumiakit`: a client of its own on the saved instance calls the core's `notificationContent`, the same call as the iOS extension's, and posts the message as a notification whose tap opens the conversation (the activity is one task; the intent's channel and kind become the target the shell opens). The writer lock is in-process on Android (`writer.rs` keeps readers and a writer per path where the file lock is unsupported), since the receiver's client and the screens' client share the stores in one process; and the call now lets go of its own client's shared hold while it works, which the iOS extension's path needed too (the stand-in had called `background()` first). The instance's UnifiedPush body names the instance, as the relay's payload does. The CLI gained `send --mention` and `whoami` for the smoke, which ends with a mention from a second account (a member through the instance's context from `mix run`, there being no invite yet), the stand-in launch posting the notification with the message, and the notification's intent opening the channel. What stays open: FCM registration once a Firebase project exists, and the Play Store build (the real distributor is checked below, 2026-11-06).
- [x] The Android release build (2026-11-04): a signed APK attached to the GitHub Release, the Play Store not being a requirement (architecture 13, "Distribution", amended: push needs no store, and an installer such as Obtainium follows the release). `scripts/release.sh [version]` builds the core optimized for arm64 alone and strips it after the bindings are generated (cargo-ndk no longer strips, and a profile strip takes the UniFFI metadata the generator reads), then `assembleRelease` with the version and the commit count as `-PlumiaVersion` and `-PlumiaBuild`, signed with the keystore the `ANDROID_KEYSTORE_*` variables name, into `.build/dist/Lumia-<version>.apk`, verified with `apksigner`. R8 is on with the bindings and JNA kept (`proguard-rules.pro`), resources shrunk, and the release ABI filtered to arm64, which took the APK from 366 MB (debug, two ABIs) through 59 MB (release unshrunk: a 44 MB dex over a 14 MB core) to 16 MB. The release lint refused `registerForActivityResult` beside the fragment library Play Services base carries, so a constraint lifts that transitive. `release.yml` gained the `android` job (the same steps as `android.yml`, in release, from the `ANDROID_KEYSTORE`, `ANDROID_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD`, `ANDROID_KEY_ALIAS` and `ANDROID_KEY_PASSWORD` secrets, with `GOOGLE_SERVICES_JSON` optional for FCM), and it gates `publish` as the desktop jobs do. Checked here with a throwaway keystore: the APK installed on the emulator, and the shrunk build walked the real screens (the trust card, password sign-in, the notification permission, Home with the seeded server, connected). Before the first tag: a release keystore (`keytool -genkeypair -keystore lumia-release.jks -alias lumia -keyalg RSA -keysize 4096 -validity 36500`), kept for the app's life since Android installs an update only over the same key, and its four secrets.
- [x] Invites (2026-11-05), the join flow of architecture 6.1 on every surface, deferred from step 2 and the one thing between the working apps and a second person: `Lumia.Invites` with its `invites` table (a code of ten letters and digits from strong randomness, the server, an optional channel, expiry and use count with 0 unlimited, guest and guest days), minted with `create_invites` through `Servers.actor` (now public), previewed in public as `InvitePreview` with the server, the count and the trust card, accepted under a row lock (a member again gets the membership back without a use), revoked by the creator or `manage_server`; `CreateAccountRequest.invite_code` joins in the same request and admits when registration is closed, on both the password and the identity-proof paths, and an unknown code makes no account. The protocol's invite table gained those semantics and the link shape `https://{host}/invite/{code}`, which the instance's UI route answers. The core: `invite_preview`, `create_invite` and `accept_invite` on `App` (a link or a bare code both give the code), `create_account` taking the invite, on the FFI and wasm; the CLI's `invite-create`, `invite`, `join`, `invite-revoke` and `account create --invite`. The clients, all alike: Home's "Join a server" (paste the link, the preview first, then join and land in the server) and the server sidebar's "Invite people" (the link made on arrival, copy, share on the phones) on the web, macOS, iOS and Android; the web's `/invite/$code` page shows the server and the instance before anything is sent and hands the sign-in page the code, so a new account is created into the server. Checked: eleven instance tests on the API; the CLI end to end against the dev instance (mint, preview by link without signing in, join, create with the code, the spent code refused); the browser suite's new step (a fresh Chromium opens the link, creates an account from it and lands in `#general`); the iOS and Android smokes screenshot both screens, and the Android smoke's second account now joins through an invite instead of the instance's context from `mix run`. Not here: rate limits on minting and accepting (architecture 7, Postgres-backed), an invites list in the UI with revocation (the CLI revokes by code; a list is the context's only), expiry and use-limit choices in the UI (the CLI takes them), and the email invite of 6.2.

## Step 8: a first release

Step 7 closed the order architecture 18 set: one secure lifecycle, then every shell. What is left is what a first release needs beyond it, gathered here (2026-11-05) from the checkboxes earlier steps parked and from what building invites and the release paths showed. In the order to take them: what a new person hits first, then what an operator needs, then the proofs the specs still owe.

The first person in:
- [x] Servers and channels from the apps (2026-11-05). `create_server` and `create_channel` on `App`, the FFI and wasm, over the API that existed (channel creation now checks `manage_channels`, which it had not). The rail's "+" on every client opens "Add a server", one page with two cards, "Create a server" and "Join with an invite" (the join page became this); the server sidebar gained "New channel" beside "Invite people": inline on the web and a popover on macOS, a pushed panel on the phones, opening the channel once made. The empty states no longer send people to the CLI. Checked: the browser suite's new step makes a server from the rail and its first channel from the sidebar and lands in it; the iOS and Android smokes screenshot the add and new-channel screens.
- [x] Invites the rest of the way (2026-11-05): `GET /servers/{id}/invites` (`ListInvitesResponse`, `create_invites`) and rate limits on minting and accepting, counted from the rows themselves (the invites and the audit log's accepts, per account per hour; `:invite_limits` overrides them, as the tests do) so every node shares one count, `RATE_LIMITED` past them. The core, the FFI, wasm and the CLI (`invites`) list and revoke. "Invite people" on every client now takes an expiry (never, a day, a week) and a use limit (unlimited, once, ten) before making the link, and lists the server's invites with their uses and expiry, each with a revoke: inline in the web sidebar, the macOS popover, and the phones' panel. The browser suite's create step makes a one-use link from the sidebar, sees the instance answer for it, revokes it and sees the instance stop. The email invite of architecture 6.2 (2026-11-06): `POST /invites/email` mints a one-use invite good for a week with the address on it and mails the link to this instance's web page (`FAILED_PRECONDITION` when the instance sends no mail, the same permission and limit as a link); the core's `email_invite` on the FFI and wasm; every client's invite panel takes an address beside the link controls and lists the invite with its address; the web suite's create step emails one, finds its link in the dev mailbox and sees it listed.2, and a limit on anonymous previews by address (needs a table; the preview gives away only what the link already names).
- [x] Presence (2026-11-05), per server as architecture 3 and protocol 6.7 say: the gateway keeps one `Phoenix.Presence` topic per server and tracks a session there only while its `PresenceUpdate` names the server (membership checked; the set replaces the last), answers a newly watched server with everyone present as `joined` and then the topic's diffs as `PresenceDiff` frames (an account's entry is the most present of its devices; a status change is `updated`, the last device leaving is `left_account_ids`); two channel tests cover the comings and goings and a server the account is not in. The core's session sends the update and raises the diff as a notice; `App::set_presence(status, watch_server_ids)` and a `PresenceDiff` event on the FFI and wasm. Every client watches the server on screen and re-sends on each welcome (a connection starts with nothing watched); the member lists on the web and macOS group members into online and offline with a status dot, and the phones, which have no member list yet, still count as present. The browser suite's create step sees the page itself online in its new server. Not here: the friends topic for Home. Away and do-not-disturb chosen by the person, with a custom status (2026-11-06): the core keeps the choice in the instance store (`status`, `set_status`; the watch call `watch_presence` sends it with the servers on screen, and every welcome re-sends it), every client's profile screen has a Status card (the three choices and a custom status line), the web and macOS member lists show the custom status beside the handle, and the web suite's link step chooses Away with a custom status.
- [x] Magic-link sign-in (2026-11-05), the first of the sign-in methods beyond a password (architecture 6.3): `POST /auth/sign-in/magic-link` mails an account's address a link (`/sign-in?magic={token}` on the instance) and the token as a code, answering 202 whether or not the address has an account, five an hour per address; `POST /auth/sign-in/magic-link/complete` signs the device in once within fifteen minutes (`magic_links` holds only the token's hash). `InstanceInfo.magic_link_enabled` says whether the instance sends mail (`LUMIA_MAGIC_LINK` in production, on in development and test where Swoosh's local mailbox takes the mail). The core's `request_magic_link` and `sign_in_magic_link` (a pasted code or the whole link), on the FFI, wasm and the CLI (`magic-link <email>`, `login --magic <token>`; `account create --email`). Every sign-in form offers "Email me a sign-in link" when the instance does, then a field for the code; the web page completes the link's `magic` query by itself. Tests: the context (once, expiry, no mail for an unknown address, the limit) and the API; the browser suite asks for a link, takes the token from the dev mailbox and signs in by the link.
- [x] Passkeys (2026-11-05), on the instance and the web: `Lumia.Auth.Passkeys` over `Wax` with the ceremonies in the browser's own JSON (`/auth/passkey/register/start` and `finish` while signed in, `/auth/passkey/assert/start` and `finish` in public, the list and a delete), the relying party the instance's host and the accepted origins its own URL and the trusted web origins, attestation not required, a challenge kept five minutes and single use, a signature count that does not move refused as a clone; the core's calls on the FFI and wasm. The web's Devices page makes and removes passkeys and the sign-in page offers "Sign in with a passkey" where the browser can; `passkeys.ts` is the ceremony between the instance's options and its check. The browser suite registers a passkey through Chromium's virtual authenticator, signs in with it and removes it. Not here: the native apps' passkey screens (the calls exist; iOS and macOS want `AuthenticationServices`, Android the Credential Manager), The passkey as the second factor (2026-11-06): `PUT /auth/passkeys/required` makes a password sign-in answer `FAILED_PRECONDITION` with `details.mfa` `passkey` until it carries an assertion by one of the account's passkeys (`passkey_challenge_id` and `passkey_credential_json`; the list says whether it is required); the core's `sign_in_with_passkey` and the requirement calls on the FFI and wasm, the wasm's errors now carrying `details` so the page reads `mfa`; the web Devices page has the switch and the sign-in page runs the ceremony and signs in again with its answer; the native apps point at the browser sign-in, as architecture 6.3 has them do for passkeys. The suite requires it, signs in with the password and the virtual authenticator, and releases it.
- [x] OIDC as a client (2026-11-05): `Lumia.Auth.Oidc` over `Assent` for the one provider the operator names (`LUMIA_OIDC_ISSUER`, `_CLIENT_ID`, `_CLIENT_SECRET`, `_NAME`), the code flow with PKCE and a nonce, the flow kept ten minutes by its state, the ID token verified against the provider's keys, the account bound to the provider's subject or made from the claims (a handle made unique, an email only when no account has it; an email never matches an existing account), and the end a one-time sign-in token the web page completes as a magic link, so no new client path was needed; `InstanceInfo.oidc_name` names the button. Tests run against a provider of their own on Bandit signing ID tokens with JOSE; the browser suite carries a provider too (the step runs when the instance names it). Not here: the native apps (they would open the start URL in the system browser and take the token back by a custom scheme), and the egress filter of architecture 7 on the issuer (operator-configured, not user-supplied).
- [x] Profile import (2026-11-05): a `SignedProfile` the identity carries, signed by this device's key over a `ProfileBody` (display name, pronouns, bio, avatar, a version that follows the last), kept beside the roster and sent with `CreateAccountRequest.profile` at creation by identity proof, where the instance verifies it against the proof's roster (the signer a device of it, not revoked) and takes the display name and avatar, keeping the bytes as the account's profile (the snapshot's own `Account.profile`); `PUT /account/profile` takes a newer one later, vouched for by the held roster, with `ProfileUpdated` on the account log. The core's `identity_profile` and `identity_set_profile` on the FFI and wasm, and the CLI's `identity profile`. Tests: import at creation, a newer version replacing it, an older one refused as a conflict, a stranger's signature refused. Not here: a profile screen in the clients (the calls exist; the CLI sets one), pronouns and bio shown anywhere, and the avatar blob itself (architecture 12's blobs are not built).
- [x] The native apps' passkey and OIDC sign-in, and a profile screen on every client (2026-11-06). Not the platform passkey APIs the entries above assumed: `AuthenticationServices` ties an app to the relying party's domain through its associated-domains entitlement (and the Credential Manager to an asset link the host serves naming the app's signing key), which an app for any instance cannot carry. The apps sign in **through the instance's web page or its provider in a system browser sheet** (`ASWebAuthenticationSession` on iOS and macOS, a Custom Tab on Android) that returns on the apps' `lumia:` scheme with a one-time token: `POST /auth/handoff` mints one for the caller's account (`HandoffResponse`, fifteen minutes, completed at the magic-link endpoint), the web sign-in page hands its sign-in to a `return` URL that way (its own origin or `lumia://`, nothing else) and forgets its own session, and the OIDC start accepts the scheme as a return. The same handoff in the other direction opens the web page signed in as the app's account to make a passkey on its Devices page (`?next=devices`). The core: `handoff_token`, `oidc_start_url`, `web_sign_in_url`, `web_handoff_url` on `App`, the FFI and wasm. The profile screen on web (the rail's self button, `view=profile`), macOS, iOS and Android: display name, pronouns and bio over `identity_set_profile`, with a notice for an account without an identity; the core now shows the profile the instance holds when this device signed none, and a new version passes the held one, so a second device of the identity can set it (it got `CONFLICT` before). The web suite: the profile set after linking and shown by the rail, and a handoff step (sign in for a return URL, the token taken to a fresh page which lands on Devices, the spent token refused through the CLI); `LUMIA_E2E_ONLY` runs named steps. The iOS and Android screen smokes carry `profile`. Architecture 6.3 says the native way. Not here: a design board for the profile screen (drawn from the Devices page's card), the avatar. Pronouns and bio shown on other people (2026-11-06): the instance keeps them from the verified profile as it keeps the display name (`accounts.pronouns`, `accounts.bio`, served on every `Account`), the core's member summary carries them, the web and macOS member lists show the pronouns beside the handle, every conversation header shows the other side's pronouns, and the web conversation's details list each person's pronouns and bio.
- [x] The JSON overrides (2026-11-06, protocol §2): `Lumia.ProtoJSON` lays the protocol's rules over the library's ProtoJSON by walking each message's field metadata, so every `bytes` id (`_id`, `_ids`), content address and nonce is a UUID string (sixteen bytes) or lowercase hex (a 32-byte identity id or content address, a 24-byte nonce) both ways, and decoding still takes stock base64 in those fields; the HTTP layer, the gateway's JSON mode and the tests go through it. Rules 2 to 4 were ProtoJSON's own. The first-party clients speak protobuf and are untouched.

An operator's instance:
- [x] A real UnifiedPush distributor against the emulator (2026-11-06): ntfy (the F-Droid build) as the distributor and ntfy.sh as its server. `scripts/smoke-unifiedpush.sh` in `apps/android` installs ntfy, opens it once (a stopped app gets no broadcasts), signs the app in on a plain launch (the shell registers for push only without a dev screen), reads the endpoint from the distributor's log, takes the app away as the OS would (`am kill`, not a force-stop, which would stop its receivers) and waits for its session to lapse (the instance pushes only to offline devices), then has a second account mention the phone's and finds the message in the posted notification. Found on the way: the receiver syncs only when the notice names its saved instance, and a dev instance calls itself `localhost` while the emulator reaches it at 10.0.2.2, so the script wants an instance started with `LUMIA_HOST=10.0.2.2` (a second one, on a database of its own); and the connector warned that the notice was plain text, so the instance now encrypts a UnifiedPush notice as Web Push under the keys the distributor made for the endpoint (the app sends them with the subscription, the FFI's `push_subscribe` taking them; a subscription without keys still gets plain JSON, with a test for each). Needs the internet, so it is run by hand, not by CI.
- [ ] The release path exercised by a tag: the Android keystore and its secrets, a deploy of the relay and an instance, the APNs key for the relay, and FCM once a Firebase project exists.
- [x] Two instances in compose (2026-11-05): `deploy/compose.two.yml`, the flagship (the directory being its account table) and a community instance from one image with a Postgres each, and `deploy/two-instances.sh`, which builds the image, brings them up and runs the cross-instance cases with the CLI (one identity with an account on each by proof and its profile with it, an invite on the community joined by a second identity that posts, the scenario suite against each). The instance workflow runs it. Step 4's checkbox on the same is closed by this.
- [x] The Linux runner's Chromium "wedge" (step 6): Bun 1.3.x cutting the DevTools pipe, fixed by Bun 1.4.4 (2026-11-06, the step 6 entry).
- [x] The macOS job's push smoke (2026-11-06): its tap stage, added with the tapped notification on 2026-11-03, had never passed on the runner. The banner did arrive, on the home screen: the script pushed 20 s after starting the UI test, and the runner takes longer than that to have the app in the foreground, so the banner was gone when the test looked (`push.png` in the run's artifact showed it). The test now announces the app in the foreground through a file (`LUMIA_UITEST_READY`) and the script pushes on that; the tap test's log joins the failure artifact. Passes locally end to end against the dev instance (which needed the app's core rebuilt: a core older than the re-wrap commit left the phone outside the group). Still failing on the runner on 2026-11-06 evening, and the info-level logs said why the banner came late: the stand-in payload carried `mutable-content` as the relay's does, the simulator cannot launch the service extension, and SpringBoard waited the extension's whole 30 s budget before presenting; the stand-in payload no longer carries the flag (the extension's rewrite is what the earlier stage checks).

The proofs the specs owe:
- [x] The named suite (2026-11-05): `mix test.failure_injection` in `apps/instance`. `Lumia.FailureInjection` reads the three lists from the spec files themselves, each covering test carries `@tag failure_injection: [delivery: 5]` (one test may cover several cases), and `Lumia.FailureInjectionTest` fails when a listed case has no tagged test and is not named as pending with its reason. 31 of the 35 cases are covered on the instance; the client halves of four (the generation bump, the snapshot under writes, the revoke while unreachable, the phrase recovery) are named with the core test or CLI scenario that holds them. New tests for the gaps: a kick on the other node ending a session's subscription here, overwrites changed on the other node during a partition, the same command on both nodes at once, a snapshot under writes from the other node, a proof replayed on the other node, the threshold at exactly 1,000 (replay) and 1,001 (snapshot), a clean resume after the slow-consumer disconnect, an administrator refused the owner's kick, an instance admin without membership refused a private channel, a certificate signed after its signer's revocation, a root rotation accepted by an instance holding the pre-rotation roster. One server bug found by them and fixed: a page replayed from Postgres (a gap fill, a subscribe replay, a reconciliation nudge) was delivered without the `acl_version` and staleness check that live delivery makes, so an overwrite committed on another node during a partition was not applied here after the heal; delivery §6 now says the rule covers replayed pages. Pending in the suite, by name: bot token scopes (authorization §8, not built), the override endpoints (authorization §7, not built), concurrent forks and the merge rule (identity §5), the client's safety-number change on rotation.
- [x] Concurrent forks and the merge rule (2026-11-05, identity §5). `lumia-core` `identity::roster::merge`: two forks become `max + 1`, devices the union minus any device revoked in either, revocations the union, rotations the longer chain with the shorter as its prefix or the forks are irreconcilable; a device certified by a signer at or after that signer's revocation in the other fork is revoked by the merge, signed by the merging device, because the verifier would refuse its certificate and monotonicity forbids dropping it (the spec gained this rule). The client's `adopt_held` adopts a newer successor as before and merges anything else that verifies on its own, refusing a fork that revokes this device; the merged roster is stored and reaches each instance with the device's next proof there. The instance applies the same-version rule: a ROOT-signed roster replaces a DEVICE-signed one it holds without the monotonicity step (recovery beats a thief's fork), everything else at the held version is `CONFLICT` with the held bytes. Tests: the merge of two additions, a revocation winning and taking the signer's later addition with it, diverging rotations refused, one rotation merged (core); a held fork merged and a newer successor adopted (client); two forks at one version with the merged successor accepted, root beating device and never the reverse (instance, identity §12 case 3 in the named suite); `deploy/two-instances.sh` has two devices of one identity add a device each at different instances, the first device merge at its next proof, and every device and both instances end on the merged v4. The client's safety-number change on root rotation: `lumia-core` `identity::safety` computes the number of the spec's section 2 (the two identities' ids and root keys sorted, BLAKE3, sixty digits in fives) and its test rotates a root and sees the number change while the id does not (2026-11-06); the named suite points at it. Not here: a client that pins keys and says so when the number changes.
- [x] Root rotation and the e2ee fault cases (2026-11-05). `Device::rotate` (identity §4.5): from the old phrase a new one, the rotation signed by the old root, the roster re-signed under the new root with every certificate as it was, the new backup key in the keystore; `lumia-cli identity rotate --phrase` publishes everywhere, re-wraps every chunk key in this device's manifest on each instance under the new backup key (`DmEngine::rewrap_backup`, e2ee §9: chunks untouched, manifest version bumped) and rewrites the identity backup. Recovery after a rotation: the phrase derives the current root, not the first, so `recover` asks `GET /identity/rosters/by-root/{root}` (public, new) and builds on what comes back; a rotated-away phrase is refused by name. The new backup key reaches the identity's other native devices by the account-log message of identity §4.5 step 5 (2026-11-06): `lumia-core` `identity::handoff` seals it to each device's key and signs the whole with the new root, `rotate` builds it, the CLI publishes it to every instance (`POST /identity/backup-key-handoff`, the instance checking the held roster's version and root before appending `BackupKeyHandedOff` to every bound account's log), and the engine opens this device's copy against the rotated roster and the app or CLI keeps it; the `rotate-handoff` scenario has b end with a's new key and a device linked afterwards with no copy. Before this, only the rotating device could restore. The e2ee §12 cases that need a second process or a fault run in `apps/core/scripts/dm-faults.sh` against a running instance, the CLI as every device, and in the e2e workflow after the browser suite: the crash between the MLS persist and the send (`dm send --crash-after-persist`, a hidden flag) resent from the outbox on restart with the sender recording its own message, which needed the sent operation kept in the store beside the outbox entry; the epoch moving under an unacked send, where the identical bytes are resent and decrypted under the past epoch, and a device added in between marks the message as before its join (the spec's case now says this instead of a re-encryption under a new command id); two members committing at once, which found a real hole: a `CONTROL` event at the current epoch was accepted as a welcome or proposal, so the second of two commits for one epoch was accepted too and the clients diverged. Welcomes now travel as their own kind, `EncryptedKind.WELCOME` (accepted at the current epoch, advancing nothing), a `CONTROL` is a commit and must be current plus one, and the loser reconciles again as soon as the refusal arrives, finding nothing left when the winner's commit covered it. One instance crash found on the way (a sign-in by an identity with no account here was a 500) is fixed.
- [x] The first load test (2026-11-06): `mix lumia.load` in `bench/`, [spikes/2026-11-06-load.md](spikes/2026-11-06-load.md). A fixture of 10 million events (1,000 channels, 1 KB bodies, 27 GB) seeded straight into the tables, then every row of architecture 17 the instance can measure through real WebSocket and HTTP clients: send to delivered on the row's workload (50 members, 10 background writes/s) 6 ms p50 and 18 ms p99; connect, authenticate and resume 50 channels with presence 66 ms p99; scrollback of 50 messages 3 ms p99; the frame at 177 bytes, which was the budget's error, not the frame's (the envelope and the message each carry ids and a timestamp; the budget is now 200); MLS encrypt and decrypt in a 10-member group 63 and 83 µs p99 (`mls_budget`, release). The two intervals of architecture 20 are set from measurement and recorded in 7.4, delivery 11 and authorization 10: a reconciler tick costs about 2 µs per channel moved in its window (2.4 ms for 1,053), so 5 s stays; a re-resolution is 0.3 ms p50 and is paid per stale delivery, so 10 minutes stays. CI runs `mix lumia.load --ci` (a small fixture, each p99 against its budget) and the MLS row in release. Two hazards found on the way: the dev code reloader's plug costs 8 ms on every request and is compiled in, so `LUMIA_CODE_RELOADER=false` leaves it out (`config/dev.exs`); and the first run after a bulk load has autovacuum in its p99 (54 ms on the send row, settled 18 ms). What the row for 100,000 connections found is below.
- [x] The connections row (architecture 17), measured at 10,000 sessions with the clients in a VM of their own: 585 KB of server RSS per connection in the morning against the 80 KB that 100,000 in 8 GB implies, and about 100 connects a second before the pool dropped queries; 97 KB per connection after the connect and 112 KB after traffic by the evening, and 2,000 connects a second without a dropped query (2026-11-06, [spikes/2026-11-06-load.md](spikes/2026-11-06-load.md)). What it was: the inet driver hands a freed read buffer to any socket that asks for a smaller one, and Postgrex sizes its sockets' buffers to the kernel's receive buffer, so idle gateway sockets kept half-megabyte blocks that Postgres results had filled (`socket_options: [buffer: 32_768]` on the Repo); a session watching presence listed the whole topic on every diff of a connect burst (one lookup per leaver now); a subscribe was three queries per channel (resolved per frame now: `Access.require_many`, the head from the row, no page read at the head, the topic subscribed before the head is read); the session hibernates after a subscribe frame and after 5 s idle; the client's reconnect delay is drawn from the upper half of each step and adds up to a second to a server-named wait. The bench's `--server` now accounts for memory by owner (allocator origins, referenced binaries, table slices, ports, carriers) and `--client-only` takes `--subscriptions` and `--no-presence`. The budget is not claimed: 112 KB is a third over, two process heaps of 27 KB, 28 KB of socket buffers and 18 KB of registry rows each, on a laptop whose OS keeps what a burst grew. Not here: the same run on a Linux node, and the transport's heap under `fullsweep_after`.

## Step 9: the desktop shell on Rune

Architecture 13 was amended on 2026-11-11: macOS, Windows and Linux share one shell in Rust on Rune, `apps/desktop`, with the core linked in. It replaces the SwiftUI macOS app and the Tauri shell. The first transition kept each shipping until the Rune shell reached parity on its platform; later the same day Ryan decided to remove both at once, there being no installs but testers'. macOS first.

The spikes behind the decision (2026-11-11):
- [x] The same shell (rail, sidebar, a 10,000-message list, a composer) over a seeded `lumia-client` store on Rune and on GPUI with GPUI Kit, measured on one Mac: `spikes/rune`, `spikes/gpui`, the shared store and seeder in `spikes/data`, the write-ups in `docs/spikes`. Rune: a window's first frame 300 ms after launch, a scrolled frame 0.12 ms of CPU at p50, 117 MB idle, one or two frames in a hundred begun a refresh late. GPUI: 370 ms, 0.64 ms, 118 MB, every frame on time.
- [x] Selection across messages on Rune (2026-11-11): the kit's selectable Markdown is a selection of its own by design, so a drag stops at the end of a message. A 13-line patch to `rune-kit` (`spikes/rune/patches/kit-selection-joined.patch`) lets a Markdown join the list's selection group; with it a drag runs through messages and code blocks, Cmd+A takes the whole channel, and frame costs do not move. Not adopted yet: see below.

The first slice (2026-11-11), `apps/desktop`:
- [x] The crate: its own workspace on Rune's toolchain, Rune pinned to one rev with its patched crates repeated (two at first, four since 2026-11-13), the core as a path dependency. `build.rs` compiles the shared assets in: `tokens.json` to colours, shadows, distances and type styles, the WOFF2 fonts to TrueType (`wuff`), `assets/icons` and the brand symbol to icons.
- [x] The session (`src/session.rs`), with no toolkit type in it: open an instance (stores laid out as `lumia-ffi` lays them, under the shell's own data directory), the trust card, password sign-in with the TOTP second factor, a new account, sign out; the snapshot, each server's members, the gateway loop with its events, presence for the server on screen; the open channel's newest 1,000 messages from SQLite; sends shown as pending until the store holds them, with a refusal shown on the message; a snapshot that fails is asked for again, 2 s to 30 s apart.
- [x] The screens: the instance card and the account card of sign-in with the other shells' wording; the Quiet Split with the rail (servers, the self avatar with its connection dot), the sidebar (channels, the account and Sign out at its foot), the conversation (header, day dividers, messages as Markdown, the composer) and the members panel grouped by presence. Light and dark.
- [x] Checks: eight tests of the screens through a windowless Rune runtime with no instance; `scripts/smoke.sh` against a dev instance (two CLI accounts, a server, messages; the shell signed in, connected, the messages and members in the accessibility tree, pictures of both themes drawn headlessly, a message typed into the composer and sent, `tests/live.rs` sending and a second launch reading it back from SQLite). The real window opens and stays connected on macOS.
- [x] Found on the way, in the instance: a server's channels were ordered by `position` alone, which every channel shares until someone orders them, so the list came back in heap order and reshuffled after an update. `Access.viewable_channels` now breaks the tie by id (creation order), with a test that failed before.

The rest of the SwiftUI app's screens, and the app around them (2026-11-11). Built as five parts side by side over a frame made first (the place is Home or a server; one conversation model for a channel and a personal conversation; a seam per part in the session, the window's state and the views), then joined:
- [x] Home and personal conversations: the sidebar (New message, the Requests, Devices and New tiles, conversations grouped by host), end-to-end encrypted conversations through the core's engine (reading, sending with the send pending until the log holds it, the notices for messages from before this device joined and ones it cannot decrypt, Rejoin for a device outside the group), requests (accept, decline, block), new message (open, or ask first), the conversation's details aside. Live tests with CLI devices as the other people: requests both ways, groups created by each side, messages both ways, a second launch reading both back.
- [x] Add a server (create; join by invite with its preview), new channel, invite people (expiry and use limit, copy, by email, the invites list, revoke). The window lands in what was made once the snapshot lists it. Not built: following an invite that names another instance (architecture 6.1).
- [x] Devices (the roster with this computer marked, revoke with the password, passkeys with remove and Add a passkey through the browser handoff), linking this computer to an identity (from sign-in and from Devices), the profile and the status, Sign out, and the magic link on the sign-in card.
- [x] Message bodies drawn by Lumia's own renderer for the protocol's subset (`src/ui/body`), with the list as one selection: a drag runs across messages, through lists, quotes and code blocks; a copy reads as a transcript with each message's author and time. What is outside the subset stays as typed, links open only as http or https, code is highlighted. This is the "own renderer" answer to selection across messages; the kit patch in `spikes/rune/patches` was not adopted. Frame cost is the kit widget's (0.13 ms p50, under 2 ms at worst over 1,000 messages).
- [x] The macOS app: `scripts/bundle-macos.sh` makes `Lumia.app` (`chat.lumia.desktop` at first, `chat.lumia` since the SwiftUI app went; the `lumia` scheme, the icon, ad hoc or Developer ID signing); one running copy per data directory, a later launch handing its links to the first; sign-in through the system browser for a passkey or the instance's OIDC provider, returning on `lumia:` with a state the app minted (a link is taken once, within ten minutes, for the instance it was started for, while signed out); the Account menu (Sign out, Change instance); the window's frame remembered; notifications for a personal message while the app is not in front, opening the conversation on a click; the app keeps running when its window closes.
- [x] Local-first launch and history. The core keeps the last snapshot and member lists (`App::kept_snapshot`, `kept_members`) and the shell starts from them, so a launch has its place, its channel, the messages and the names before the instance answers; a launch returns to the channel last on screen. The list holds a page of 200 and reads further back as the reader scrolls up, from the store (`App::messages_before`) and then from the instance (`App::older_messages`). A live test reads a channel of 1,100 messages back to its first on a device that was there for none of them, by the session and by scrolling the real list.
- [x] The screens drawn to the design canvas's boards (the Quiet Split and sign in, light and dark, read from their styles; nobody compared them side by side on a screen). Sign-in has the stacked logo, the card's and fields' metrics, the "or" rule and the outlined ways in; the rail, sidebar rows, members, header, message rows, composer and the panes without a board follow the same vocabulary. A first launch opens at 1280 by 900 so the sign-in card fits. What the boards draw that the product has no feature for was left out; the list of what the design still expects is in the second pass below. The design system's gaps the pass met (no tokens for border widths, the disabled look, several control heights, glyph and avatar-initial sizes, a code style and syntax colours, the stacked logo's crop) are derived in `src/tokens.rs` and `build.rs` with a comment each.
- [x] A second pass against the boards, this time by eye (2026-11-12), after Ryan said the app did not match them well enough and that the traffic lights sat too far in. The boards were drawn in a browser at 1440 by 900 and the shell headlessly at the same size, and the same regions put side by side. What the first pass had copied from the styles held up (sizes, type, colours, spacing); what it had missed or left out, and is fixed: the traffic lights are at the head of the rail where the boards now draw them (7 in, 12 down) with the sidebar back at the board's inset, where they had been 22 in each way with everything pushed down to clear them; a conversation shorter than its pane sits at the foot of the pane, against the composer; the selected server's ring is 2 points of accent outside a 2-point gap (the layers of a shadow token were painted in the wrong order, so the ring was 4 solid points); the server's name is the board's button with its chevron and opens a menu (Invite people, New channel), with the board's New message pencil beside it, where two blue links had hung under the channels; the rail's self avatar opens the board's account menu (who is signed in, the status as three radio rows, View profile, Devices, Sign out); a member's row and a message's avatar open the profile card of the new Profile card boards (banner in the person's tone, avatar over its edge, name, handle, presence, what their verified profile says, Message); the side panel has the board's "Find a member" search and its heading in the tab's words; under the composer is the board's "Return to send", and the send button is not dimmed over an empty field; the password field has the board's eye. A server whose instance reports no presence no longer repeats "Members" as a group under the heading. Checked by 150 tests and the smoke; the menus, the card and the lights' spot have been seen in headless frames only, the lights there as Rune's overlay of where AppKit will put them.
- [x] Messages in blocks, with more room between them (2026-11-12, from Ryan using 0.3.1). What one person sends close together is one block, as the boards draw a message of several paragraphs: the avatar, the name and the time over the first, the rest under it a paragraph's distance apart, each still a row of its own in the list. "Close together" is the same person on the same day within five minutes of the message before (`joins` in `src/ui/timeline.rs`), and a send on its way joins the block it will land in. A row under the first has its time in the avatar's column, shown under the pointer and always there for assistive technology with the author's name. A copy names a block once. Blocks stand 8 points apart where the boards drew 2; the boards on the canvas were changed to match. Found on the way: the 2 points had never shown, because the rows asked for them as a margin, which Rune's virtual list does not lay out; the room is padding in a slot around the row now. Checked by two tests, the `blocks` scene drawn headlessly in both themes, and the timeline benchmark (a scrolled frame 0.14 ms at p50, none over 2 ms, as before). Not seen in a window by anyone but in pictures. The web client draws blocks since the same day (step 6), and so do the iOS and Android apps (the two entries after the typing one).
- [x] The handle and the display name can be changed (2026-11-12, asked for by Ryan; architecture 6.2 "Names", decided the same day). Until now a display name could only be set by a device holding the identity's key, through the signed profile, and a handle not at all: an account made with a password on a computer with no identity had no way to a name. `PATCH /account` changes either from any signed-in device; the later of it and a signed profile is the account's name on the instance. The handle left is held for its account for 30 days. Handles had no rule at all on the instance (any string made an account): they are 2 to 32 of a to z, digits, `_`, `.` and `-` now, on creation too, and a taken one answers `ALREADY_EXISTS` as the clients had always worded it, where it had answered `INVALID_ARGUMENT`. The core has `App::update_account` (FFI `update_account`, wasm `updateAccount`). The desktop's Profile page begins with an Account card: the handle, and the display name on a computer that holds no identity (one that does keeps the name in the signed profile's card). Checked by the instance's tests, two screen tests and a live one (a rename, a taken handle refused in words, the old handle refused at sign-in and to a new account, the new one signing in). **Not done**: the iOS and the Android Profile pages (the web's has the card since the same day, step 6); an account whose handle predates the rule keeps it, can still change its name, and is held to the rule only when it changes the handle; nobody is told when someone they know renames (a member list shows it when next read).
- [x] A profile photo (2026-11-12, asked for by Ryan with the names; architecture 12 "Avatars" and the deployment spec amended first). **The instance**: `PUT /account/avatar` takes the file as chosen and keeps its own square of it, made by `native/lumia_media`, a Rust NIF on a dirty scheduler, which is what architecture 3 and 12 had decided for image work and is the first native code in the instance (it needs Rust to build now; the image and CI carry it). The format is told from the bytes, the declared size is checked before anything is decoded, and nothing of the upload is kept, so a photograph's metadata goes no further. The picture is the first **blob**: `Lumia.Blobs` seals it under a key of its own, that key under the instance master key, and names it by the keyed BLAKE3 of the ciphertext; the store is a directory or an S3-compatible bucket (`LUMIA_BLOB_STORE`, with AWS's own variables for a bucket), and an instance with no store or no master key keeps no pictures and says so. `GET /avatars/{address}` serves one to whoever has its address. A backup carries a directory store and a restore checks every blob the database names is there. **The core**: `App::set_avatar`, `remove_avatar` and `avatar` (bytes, for a shell that draws its own pictures, from this instance only), `avatar_url` on the snapshot and on every person; through the FFI and the wasm client. **The desktop**: the Account card has the photo (the system's file dialog, Change, Remove, each refusal in words), and everywhere a person is drawn their picture is over their initials; the bytes are fetched once, a few at a time, and kept under the instance's directory, so a later launch has them before the instance answers. Checked by the instance's tests (blobs, the S3 adapter against a stand-in bucket, the NIF over nine fixture files, the endpoints, backup and restore), the crate's own tests on macOS and in a Linux container, the instance image built and the NIF run inside it, the core's tests, two screen tests and a live one (a text file refused in words; a photograph set, its bytes back without the camera's name in them, kept on disk, seen by a second account in a shared server, there at a second launch before anything is drained, and removed), and a scene drawn headlessly.
- [ ] Pictures, where they are not yet. **The flagship keeps none until a release with this work deploys there**: it runs 0.3.1, which has no blob store. Its bucket and master key exist since 2026-11-12 (Ryan's: a private Cloudflare R2 bucket, chosen over Tigris and S3 on price and because Tigris is being bought by Dropbox; the six secrets are set on `lumia-instance`), and `apps/instance/fly.toml` names the key file and writes it from the secret, so the next deploy from it keeps pictures. Until that deploy, setting a photo there cannot work, and nothing has shown that the credentials, the endpoint or the key are right. The dev instance on this Mac keeps them on disk under a key that is no secret. **Never run against a real bucket**: the S3 adapter has only met a stand-in. The iOS and the Android apps neither set nor draw pictures (the web does both since the same day, step 6). Nobody has chosen a file through the dialog by hand, or seen a picture in a window; the shell with Rune's file dialog turned on was built on this Mac and type-checked for Linux in a container, and not for Windows. The signed profile does not name the picture, so it stays on the instance it was set on and does not travel with the identity; a picture a signed profile names on another host is never fetched, so it shows as initials. Since #15 (2026-11-14) everyone who shares a server or a conversation is told when someone's picture or name changes, and every client draws it at once. There is no cropping: the instance takes the middle. A picture's bytes kept on a computer are not removed when the account changes or removes it. Attachments are not built: no per-server address keys, reference counts or access checks, and an `s3://` store has no backup of its own (the bucket's versioning is the operator's to turn on). Found on the way: `mix deps.get` reports a security advisory against the locked Ecto (unbounded query cache growth from interpolated JSON paths); nothing here builds such queries from input, and the bump is not made.
- [x] Scroll bars (2026-11-12, from Ryan: "there is no scrollbar in the desktop apps on the chat window"). The shell had none anywhere: Rune's viewports scroll without one unless asked. Every pane that scrolls now has the kit's bar (`src/ui/scroll.rs`): the message list, the channel and conversation lists, the members and participants, Home's pages, the invite popover and the sign-in and link screens. It follows the system's "Show scroll bars": there whenever the content overflows under "Always" or with a mouse, and over the content while it scrolls or the pointer is at the edge under "When scrolling" or with a trackpad, which is the Mac's default and means a still window shows none. The thumb drags, a press in the track pages or jumps as the system says. Its colours are derived (the design system names none; the boards have a browser's own). Checked by a test that scrolls the list and finds the bar shown and its thumb moved, and drawn headlessly under "Always"; **not seen in a window**, where dragging the thumb and the system's own setting have not been tried.
- [x] Preferences (2026-11-12, from Ryan: "can the scroll bars be in a preferences screen that is accessed from the menu over the username/photo on the bottom left? also add the display preferences: comfortable | compact, also light|dark|system appearance"). The account menu on the rail's own avatar has the item the boards already drew there, under View profile, and it opens a page at Home (`src/ui/home/preferences.rs`); on the Mac the application menu has "Preferences…" on Command-comma as well. Three choices, each kept in `settings.json` on this computer and none of them on the account. **Appearance**: Light, Dark or System (the default, which follows the system as it changes). The window's colours come from the choice on every platform; on macOS the app's own appearance is set to match, so what AppKit draws for it agrees. **Messages**: Comfortable (as it was) or Compact, which draws the small avatar and halves the room between blocks, between a block's messages and around a day's divider; it changes the message list only, not the sidebars or the composer. **Scroll bars**: Automatic (as System Settings says), When scrolling or Always. Rune gives an app no say in this, it reads the system's setting, so on macOS the choice is carried out as the system's own setting made for this app alone (`AppleShowScrollBars` in the app's preferences, and the notice that has Rune read it again), through `crates/sparkle` because that is the crate that may write the message sends. **On Windows and Linux the scroll bar card is not drawn**: Rune shows them one way there and a control that did nothing would be a lie. There is no board for the page; it is the Profile page's cards with the design system's segmented choice. Checked by three tests (the menu leads there and each choice is kept across a launch; light, dark and system colour the shell; compact takes less room) and two headless pictures. The two that go through AppKit only happen in a real launch, and one was made of the debug binary with Dark and Always in its settings and again with the defaults: its trace (`LUMIA_DEV_TRACE`) says both were set each time, and `AppleShowScrollBars` was written to its preferences and then removed. **Not seen in a window**: nobody has looked at one, so that the bars are then drawn, and that a change on the page takes effect at once and not at the next launch, are not known. The web and the phones got the page the same day (the next entry).
- [x] Preferences on the web, iOS and Android (2026-11-12, from Ryan: "add to the web version and ios and android"; the web in the main checkout, the phones by two agents each in a worktree of its own). The same page with the first two choices, in the desktop's words: **Appearance** (Light, Dark, System, the default) and **Messages** (Comfortable, the default and exactly what was drawn before, or Compact: the small avatar and half the room between blocks, inside one and around a day's divider, in the message list only). Each is the device's and not the account's, takes effect as it is chosen, is kept through a relaunch and a sign-out, and reads as the default when what is stored is not understood: `localStorage` in the browser (`lumia:preferences`, followed from another tab), `UserDefaults` on iOS, the `lumia` `SharedPreferences` on Android, under the desktop's value names. **The scroll bar choice is the Mac's alone**: a page has no say in its scroll bars that holds in every browser, and a phone's indicators are the system's. **Web**: the account menu has the item the boards drew (View profile, Preferences, Devices, Sign out); a chosen appearance is `data-theme` on the root and System is no attribute; the gallery gives the page back to the preference when left and shows the compact block. A step of the browser suite goes through it in Chromium and WebKit (each appearance against the system's, a block compact, both kept through a reload and a sign-out), and the whole suite passed. **The phones had no account menu**: the rail's own avatar went straight to Profile. It now opens one on both (View profile, Preferences, Devices), so Profile is a tap further; the glyph is the system's sliders (SF Symbols, Material), not `assets/icons/preferences.svg`. On both, the rules are values in the kit with unit tests (iOS 10 new of 27, Android 6 new of 19), a debug launch can name a choice for that launch without storing it (`LUMIA_DEV_APPEARANCE`, `LUMIA_DEV_DENSITY`), the smokes draw the screen light and dark and the channel compact, and a build from before the work drawn beside the final one showed Comfortable unchanged pixel for pixel. On iOS `scripts/smoke-preferences.sh` is new (a UI test goes through the menu, reads each choice's state, chooses, relaunches); Android's `smoke.sh` does the same in itself. On iOS two of a block's messages stand 6 apart when compact, not 4: a `Text` has no leading in its box, so the row adds it back at either density. **Fixed on the way**: iOS cut "Do not disturb" short in Profile's status tabs, and those tabs said nothing of which was chosen to VoiceOver; Android rebuilt its activity when the phone went dark or light, which sent whoever was reading back to Home (it now takes the change itself); both smokes take `LUMIA_CLI`. **Not verified**: no real phone, and nobody has listened to VoiceOver or TalkBack (the choices' states were read from the accessibility trees); one simulator (iPhone 15 Pro, iOS 17.4) and one emulator (API 35); no release build of either, so that it ignores the two debug names rests on the debug guard; a personal conversation compact was drawn on iOS and not on Android; the iOS smokes' five more launches and the new script are not in the macOS workflow. **Known and left**: with an appearance chosen against the system's, each client may show the system's for a moment at launch (the web before its script runs, the phones' launch windows, Android's being light always); what the system draws over the phones' apps (keyboard, share sheet, the browser tab) follows the phone and not the choice.
- [x] Who is typing, in a server's channel (2026-11-12, from Ryan using 0.3.1; protocol 6.7, which gained what the server does). The instance had accepted `Typing` and ignored it: it now fans it out to the channel's other subscribers as `TypingIndicator` eight seconds ahead, only from a subscriber who may post there, never for a personal conversation, never to the sender's own account, and not twice within three seconds for a channel; nothing is stored. The core sends it (`App::typing`, called on every edit and spaced to one in five seconds, held back for anything the kept snapshot does not list as a server's channel) and reports it (`AppEvent::Typing`, its end moved onto this device's clock by the welcome's `server_time` and never more than ten seconds off). The shell names who is typing under the composer as the boards do, three dots and "Grace Hopper is typing…", two people by name, "Several people" beyond that; a message from someone ends theirs at once, and silence ends it when its time runs out, for which the window asks to be woken. `lumia-cli typing <channel>` says it once and `tail` prints it. Checked by the instance's tests (five; the tests of who may post and of personal conversations were seen to fail with their guard taken out), the core's, a screen test, and `tests/live_typing.rs`: two shells in one channel of an instance built from this tree, each seeing the other type, a message ending it, and silence ending it after about eight seconds. Seen in headless frames only; the dots have not been seen moving.
- [x] A refresh that fails says nothing over the composer (2026-11-12, from Ryan using 0.3.1 against the flagship: "transport: error sending request for url (…/account/snapshot)" in red under a message that had been sent). The shell asks for the account snapshot again on every welcome and account event; one of those requests got no answer, the failure was written where a failed send is, nothing asked again, and the line stayed until the next welcome. With a snapshot held, a failed refresh now leaves the screen as it was and is asked for again after 2 s, then 4, up to 30, as a first load already was; the rail's dot is what says the gateway is away. A session the instance no longer knows, and a first load with nothing kept, say so as before. Checked by a screen test seen to fail without the change. Why the request failed that once is not known: the instance answered a minute later, and nobody looked at its logs.
- [x] Nobody is signed out for doing nothing, and a session that has ended says so (2026-11-12, from a tester on 0.3.1 against the flagship: "session revoked" in red over the composer, in a shell where nothing worked any more). Two faults, one behind the other. **The core could get its own session revoked**: a refresh token is single use and presenting a retired one revokes the session (protocol 12), and `Api::refresh_now` was not serialised, so when the 15-minute access token had run out and two calls were refused together (a welcome asks for the snapshot and the members at once), both presented the same refresh token and the second was a reuse. The clones of `Api` now rotate one at a time, and a call that waited behind another's rotation takes the pair that one brought back. **And every shell took only `UNAUTHENTICATED` for a session that is over**, so `SESSION_REVOKED`, `DEVICE_REVOKED` and a refresh token run out left the person where they were with the instance's words in red: the desktop shell and the web app now go to sign-in for all four, and the card says "You were signed out of this computer" (or browser). Beside them: the gateway loop ended for good when a rotation got no answer at all, where only the instance's refusal should end it, and in the browser it never ended on a refusal; a loop that has ended makes both shells ask for the snapshot, which either says the session is over or, later, starts the loop again. Checked by two tests in the core against a small local instance (both seen to fail without the lock: the same token presented twice), a screen test on the desktop, and a step of the browser suite that revokes the browser's session from elsewhere and sees it sent back to sign-in, in Chromium and WebKit. The phones had the same four-way mistake: iOS is fixed the same day (the sign-in card says "You were signed out of this phone"; a session test against a running instance revokes the phone's session from elsewhere, and it was seen once in a simulator) and so is Android (the entry on the phones below). **Not done**: protocol 6.8 says a client told its session is revoked must wipe the local store for that instance, and no client does. The one way left to lose a session for nothing, a rotation whose answer never arrives, is closed by the grace in the entry below.
- [x] A lost answer does not cost a session (2026-11-12, Ryan's decision the same day; protocol 12 and architecture 6.3 amended). For 60 seconds after a rotation, the refresh token it retired is answered with the same pair again and nothing about the session moves; after that, presenting it is the reuse that revokes, as before. The instance keeps only hashes, so to say a pair again it has to be able to make it again: a rotation now derives the new access and refresh tokens from the one presented (HMAC under a key from the instance's secret, by session and purpose) instead of drawing them at random, and the session row gained the time its previous token was retired. This also covers the clients already out (0.3.1 and the phones), whose two racing refreshes now get one pair between them instead of one of them a revocation. The grace reaches one rotation back and no further, and a row rotated before the column existed has none. Checked by the instance's tests (the lost answer, the race, the grace's end, two rotations back, an older row; 308 in all). Not checked against a real dropped connection, and the flagship has it only once a release deploys there and its migration runs.
- [x] Two processes on one session do not cost it either (2026-11-12, found by reading while the phone was being fixed, not seen happen). A phone's notification extension, and Android's push receivers, open a client of their own on the app's stores. When a push arrives with the access token run out, that client rotates the pair and writes the new one to the keys store; the app, which read the pair once when it opened, still holds the retired refresh token and presents it at its next refusal, which past the grace is the reuse that revokes. The core now reads the stored pair again before it presents a refresh token (`Api::stored`, wired in `App::open` beside the rotation hook) and takes it when someone else has changed it there, rotating it in turn only if it has run out too; a pair this process failed to write is not mistaken for another's. And since a gateway loop that has ended now asks for the snapshot, which starts it again, a loop that ended within ten seconds of starting is held back until the window (or the page, or the phone's app) is looked at again: two clients on one session knock each other off, the instance closing the first connection for the second, and each starting again at once would go on for ever. Checked by a test in the core against the small local instance (the stored pair is taken, the retired one is never presented), the desktop's 163 tests and its smoke with the live tests against an instance built from this tree, and the browser suite. Not checked: an extension and an app actually racing on a phone, and the hold itself, which nothing here provokes.
- [x] The phones send an ended session back to sign-in too (2026-11-12, Ryan asked for it after the desktop and the web; two agents, one in `apps/apple` and one in `apps/android`, each in a worktree of its own). On both, one rule says whether a failure means the session is over (the four codes, the core's "device revoked" and "not signed in"; `lumiaSignedOut` in LumiaKit, `sessionOver` and `endsSession` in the Android kit, each with a unit test that pins the codes); the load and every refresh of the snapshot end the session through the app's own sign-out when the rule says so, and the sign-in card says once "You were signed out of this phone. Sign in again to carry on."; any other failure of a snapshot that is held says nothing; and a gateway loop that has ended asks for the snapshot once, which starts it again unless it had only just begun (ten seconds on iOS, thirty on Android, ten on the desktop and the web: three numbers for one idea, to be made one). On iOS a loop that had ended is also started again when the app comes to the foreground; on Android a launch on a revoked session no longer asks for the notification permission over the sign-in screen, and the sign-out it runs finishes even when the refusal arrived on the gateway's own job. Checked on iOS by `swift test` (17, with a session test that needs `LUMIA_TEST_INSTANCE` and is skipped without it: an account made, its session revoked from elsewhere, the session ended and the line there once; seen to fail with the old rule), the simulator build, `smoke-ios.sh` and `smoke-push.sh` in an iPhone 17 simulator, and once by eye there. Checked on Android by the kit's 13 unit tests with the debug build, and on an API 35 emulator with a script that revokes the phone's session: back to sign-in with the line whether the app was running or not, where the build before stayed in the shell; the smoke gained that as its last step. **Not run to its end**: Android's `scripts/smoke.sh`, which stops at its typing step against the dev instance on this Mac (0.2.1, older than typing) with or without this change, and passed with that one check set aside. Not checked on either: a device; `DEVICE_REVOKED` and a refresh token run out, which only the unit tests hold; a loop that ends while the session is good.
- [x] The phones can sign out (2026-11-12, Ryan: "add both", after the Preferences work found that `AppStore.signOut()` was called from no screen on either; two agents, one app and one worktree each). "Sign out" is the last item of the account menu on the rail's own avatar, after a rule, with no confirmation, as on the web and the desktop. **It takes the phone's push subscription back first**, while the session can still say so: the instance keeps subscriptions by device and not by session, so a signed-out phone would have gone on getting the account's notices (`apns` on iOS; on Android each platform the phone subscribed as). A failure there does not stop the rest: the session is revoked, the tokens and what was held of the account are forgotten, and the sign-in screen opens on the saved instance with nothing said (the "You were signed out of this phone" line stays for a session the instance ended). Preferences, the identity and the device keys stay. A second tap does nothing: one rule on each phone runs a leaving once, whether chosen or ended (`leaving` in LumiaKit's session, `Leaving.kt` on Android), and it is seen through if the screen that asked goes away. **What remembers "this token was sent" forgets at sign-out** (`PushLedger` on iOS, the `push` marks on Android), so the next sign-in subscribes again; iOS also unregisters from APNs. Checked: unit tests of the order and of once-only (iOS 5 new of 32, Android 7 new of 26); on iOS `scripts/smoke-signout.sh`, new, where the simulator got a real APNs token: the session gone from the instance and the `relay_apns` row gone from `push_subscriptions` after each sign-out, both back after signing in again, the next launch on sign-in; on Android in `smoke.sh`, with a listener standing in for a UnifiedPush endpoint (`LUMIA_DEV_PUSH`, debug only): a notice before, none for a mention while signed out, one after signing in again. **Fixed on the way**: iOS marked a token sent before the call, so a send that failed was never tried again; Android posted "New message" for a push that reached a signed-out phone, and a session the instance ended left its mark behind, so the next account on that phone was never subscribed. **Not verified**: no real phone, so APNs' own unregister and the notification extension after a sign-out; nothing of FCM (no `google-services.json`); a real UnifiedPush distributor's delivery; signing out with no network and the second tap, which are unit tests only; signing in as a different account afterwards; VoiceOver and TalkBack by ear. `smoke-signout.sh` is not in the macOS workflow, and Android's `smoke.sh` now runs close to ten minutes. What it found in the core, the instance and the apps was fixed the same day (the next entry).
- [x] What signing out left open (2026-11-12, Ryan: "fix the issues"; the core and the instance in the main checkout, the phones by two agents, each in a worktree). **The core** (8b30399, 49e9844): every request has a time to be answered in (20 seconds, a second more per 16 KiB sent, two minutes for a history chunk, 8 for the sign-out and for taking the push subscription back), so a sign-out against an instance that takes the connection and answers nothing reaches sign-in in 16 seconds on both phones, where it waited as long as TCP does; a sign-in over a held session keeps the new pair, then revokes the held session by its own tokens (`App::take_session`), so linking a phone after a password sign-in leaves one session, and a gateway loop running on the held one, told it was revoked, comes back on the new one instead of ending; signing out tries every delete and drops the pair whatever the store says. Two bugs the phones' runs showed: **a second account on a device never connected** (the store kept the first account's account-log cursor, the second resumed from it, its events past that were refused as a gap and the loop reconnected for ever), now that signing out forgets the account log and the unsent commands (`SqliteStore::forget_account`; those commands would have gone out as whoever signed in next) and the loop resumes from the cursor of the account the store last followed, with a welcome that names another account starting that one's log afresh; and **`App::stop()` did not stop the loop** (the event task held a sender of its own, so a signed-out device kept its gateway until the instance dropped it), now that the task reads the application's sender, and the loop also notices a stop during its backoff wait and gives an upgrade nobody answers 20 seconds. `crates/lumia-client/tests/live_app.rs` shows the four against an instance (`LUMIA_TEST_INSTANCE`), and each fails with its bug put back. **The instance** (b038efe): a device's push subscriptions are removed with its last usable session of the account, however it ends, so a phone signed out from elsewhere, revoked from the identity, or whose own unsubscribe never arrived is told nothing more. The protocol says all of this (section 12, the push subscription row). **The glyph** (a553ecb): `sign-out.svg` is the boards' door mirrored, the arrow leaving; the boards on the canvas still have the old one. **Android** (e40d3fd): leaving a session unregisters from the UnifiedPush distributor and deletes FCM's token (with ntfy on the emulator a sign-in after a sign-out got a new endpoint, where the same came back before); the one `AppStore` is the application's (`LumiaApp`), so turning the phone keeps one session, one gateway connection and the screen, during a sign-out too; a second account after signing out shows nothing of the first, holds the device's one session, and is the one mentions reach; a session revoked while the app is away takes the subscription before the phone runs. Found on the way: the sign-in screen kept the instance's answer in its saved state, which crashed the app whenever it left the foreground from sign-in (Home, or the browser a sign-in opens); `emulator.sh` now says it needs Java. `smoke.sh` runs in 358 s (385 before) and `smoke-signout.sh` is new, with `relay.py` standing in for an instance that stops answering. **iOS** (188f0f1): delivered notifications go at sign-out, chosen or ended; a second account after signing out shows nothing of the first and owns the subscription; linking leaves one session and the gateway connected on it (`smoke-push.sh` checks both on the instance); a session revoked from outside takes the subscription before the phone acts; a push to a signed-out phone with the app in front is neither shown nor listed (`willPresent`), and the extension's decision is `LumiaClient.notice`, which answers nothing without a session. Found on the way: a sign-in over a held session within ten seconds of the welcome left the phone signed in with no gateway until the next foreground, now the core's to handle. Checked: core 80 tests and the four live ones, the instance's 311, the desktop's 167 with its smoke, the web suite in both browsers, the phones' unit tests (iOS 35, Android 26) and every smoke of both, all against a scratch instance built from master. **Not verified**: no real phone; nothing of FCM (no `google-services.json`); delivery through a real distributor after re-registering; VoiceOver and TalkBack by ear; the phones' new smokes run by hand only. One run of the web suite failed once at the personal-message step in Chromium while both phones' smokes were on the same instance, and passed in the five runs after; its message was lost.
- [x] Server settings (2026-11-12, from Ryan: "the server admin needs to be able to update server settings. name, icon, etc..."; architecture 6.7, decided first; the core, the instance, the desktop shell and the web in the main checkout, the phones by two agents each in a worktree). A server's owner, or a member whose roles grant `MANAGE_SERVER`, changes its **name** (1 to 100 characters once trimmed, now at creation too), its **picture** (kept and served as an account's photo is) and **how long it keeps its messages** (the per-server retention the instance already enforced daily, which had no endpoint; Forever, 1 year, 90 days or 30 days, and a value set elsewhere shown as it is). `PATCH /servers/{id}` (the protocol had promised it, never built) and `PUT`/`DELETE /servers/{id}/icon`; each setting audited on its own; every member is told by `ServerUpdated` on its account log, from a job after the commit, in batches. The account snapshot's own memberships carry the member's resolved server-level permissions, so a client offers "Server settings" (in the menu under the server's name on the desktop and the web, a link under the channels on the phones) only to whoever may, and `ServerSummary` has `can_manage`, `icon_url` and `retention_days`. Every client draws a server's picture on its rail, and says before a shorter retention is saved: "Messages older than 30 days will be deleted for everyone. This cannot be undone." A new glyph, `settings`, a gear drawn after the set.
